## Claude

Cool — exploring mode is the right place to start. Let's build the mental model before any tactics.

Quick gut check first: when you hear "angel investor," what do you picture them actually *doing* day-to-day? And what do you think makes someone good at it vs. bad at it?

I ask because most people imagine angels as "rich people who pick winners," and that framing leads to terrible decisions. The real job is closer to something you already do every day at OSA — and once you see the parallel, the whole thing clicks differently.

Take a swing at it and I'll meet you there.

---

## Phelipe

I picture them doing meetings with founders. Studying the current state of economics and tech?

---

## Claude

You're not wrong — but that's the visible 10%. The other 90% is where the actual edge lives, and it's stuff you'd recognize.

Here's the reframe: **angel investing is dealflow + pattern matching + portfolio construction.** Same three muscles you're building at OSA right now.

- **Dealflow** — the angels who win don't pick better, they *see more*. The best deals never hit AngelList or Demo Days; they get filled by warm intros 48 hours after the founder decides to raise. Your job is to be in the top 5 names a founder texts. This is exactly the "dream 500 / dark social" game you're already running for OSA.
- **Pattern matching** — meetings with founders aren't about evaluating the idea. The idea will change. You're evaluating whether *this human* can navigate ambiguity, recruit better people than themselves, and survive 7 years of being told no. You already pattern-match this on operators and pilots. Same skill, different domain.
- **Portfolio construction** — this is the part nobody talks about and it's the whole game. Angel returns follow a power law: roughly 1 in 10–20 deals returns the entire fund, the rest go to zero or limp. So a "good" angel isn't someone who picks 8 winners out of 10. It's someone who writes 30+ checks, accepts that 25 will die, and structures so the 1–2 monsters can return 50–100x the check. If you write 5 checks, you statistically get zero. The math punishes small portfolios brutally.

The studying-economics-and-tech piece you mentioned is real but secondary — it's table stakes, not edge. Edge is access (dealflow) and judgment (pattern matching), compounded over a portfolio big enough for the power law to work.

The uncomfortable implication for someone exploring: if you can't commit to ~25–50 checks over 5–10 years (so call it $250K–$1M minimum across a real portfolio), straight angel investing is mathematically a worse vehicle than just LPing into a syndicate or a small fund where someone else does the construction for you.

Want me to go deeper on any of the three? Or we can talk about the on-ramps that don't require being accredited yet — there are a few legitimate ones, and a few that look legitimate but aren't.

## Phelipe

How much should you invest a year as angel? Can you be an LP in a fund for how much? Can you follow funds?

---

## Claude

Three good questions. Let me hit them in order.

**How much per year as an angel**

The honest answer: angel money should be money you can light on fire without it changing your life. Standard guidance is **5–10% of investable net worth max**, spread over 3–5 years of deployment so you're not concentrated in one vintage (one bad year of the market). Vintage diversification matters more than people realize — angels who deployed everything in 2021 got crushed.

Translating that to check sizes: if you want a real portfolio of 25–30 deals at $5–10K checks, you're looking at $150–300K deployed over ~5 years, plus reserves for follow-ons (some angels reserve 50% of capital for follow-ons in winners; others don't follow on at all and just write more first checks). So roughly $30–60K/year if you're going direct.

Below that, the math doesn't work and you should be an LP instead.

**LP minimums in funds**

Wide range depending on the vehicle:

- **Rolling funds (AngelList)** — quarterly subscriptions, often $5–10K/quarter minimums. Lowest friction way in. You're committing to a manager, not a deal.
- **Traditional small VC funds (sub-$50M)** — usually $100–250K minimum LP commitment, called over 3–4 years. Some emerging managers will take $50K from strategic LPs.
- **Established VC funds** — $1M+ minimums, often $5M+. Not the door you're walking through.
- **Fund-of-funds** — $250K–$1M typical, gives you exposure across many funds. Heavy fee drag (fees on fees).

All of these require accredited status, and most require **qualified purchaser** status ($5M+ in investments) for the bigger funds under the 3(c)(7) exemption.

**Can you follow funds?**

Yes, and this is actually the most interesting part of your question. Three flavors:

1. **Syndicates (AngelList, Sydecar, etc.)** — a lead investor sources a deal, opens an SPV, and lets others co-invest deal-by-deal. Minimums often $1–5K per deal. You're picking deals one at a time, riding on the lead's diligence. Carry is usually 20% to the lead. **This is the closest thing to "following funds" without being an LP.** You can follow specific leads whose taste you trust.

2. **Co-invest rights** — bigger LPs in funds sometimes get the right to invest directly alongside the fund in specific deals, often with no fees/no carry. Powerful but typically reserved for $1M+ LPs.

3. **Scout programs** — some funds (Sequoia famously) give operators small checkbooks ($25–100K) to deploy as scouts, sharing carry. You're not following the fund, the fund is using you as sensors. Invitation-only, but worth knowing exists because founder-operators with strong networks (read: you, in 2–3 years, if OSA is working) get tapped.

**The honest synthesis for where you are**

You're not accredited yet, which legally rules out almost all of the above except some Reg CF platforms (Republic, Wefunder) — and I'd be straight with you: those are mostly adverse-selected dealflow. The good deals don't need to crowdfund.

So the actually-useful move while you're "exploring" is: **build the muscle without the money**. Track 20 startups publicly, write down your investment thesis on each before you'd theoretically invest, then check yourself 18 months later. Costs nothing, builds the only skill that matters (pattern matching with feedback loops), and when OSA puts you in the room with real founders and real capital, you'll have 2 years of tracked judgment instead of starting cold.

Want to go deeper on syndicates specifically, or on building that paper-trading muscle?

## Phelipe

What is accredited?

---

## Claude

Good question to ask — it's the gate that controls almost every door we just talked about.

**Accredited investor** is an SEC designation that says you meet certain wealth or sophistication thresholds, which lets you invest in private securities (startups, VC funds, hedge funds, private real estate) that aren't registered with the SEC. The logic, fairly or not, is that these investments are riskier and less regulated, so the government restricts them to people who can theoretically afford to lose the money or are sophisticated enough to evaluate the risk.

**The thresholds for an individual** (any one of these qualifies you):

- **Income** — $200K+ in each of the last two years, with reasonable expectation of the same this year. $300K+ if combined with a spouse.
- **Net worth** — $1M+, *excluding* the value of your primary residence. Can be combined with a spouse.
- **Professional credentials** — holding an active Series 7, Series 65, or Series 82 license. (Added in 2020. The "I know what I'm doing" path that doesn't require wealth.)
- **Knowledgeable employee** — if you work at the private fund itself, you can invest in it.

**How it actually works in practice**

Nobody from the SEC checks. The investment platform or fund does the verification — usually one of three ways:

1. You upload tax returns or W-2s showing income.
2. You upload a brokerage/bank statement showing $1M+ in assets, plus a signed letter.
3. A CPA, lawyer, or registered broker-dealer writes a verification letter on your behalf (most common for net worth path; costs ~$50–200 through services like VerifyInvestor).

Verification is typically good for 90 days to a year depending on the platform.

**Why it matters for your situation**

Until you're accredited, you're locked out of:
- Direct angel investments in startups (with rare exceptions)
- AngelList syndicates and rolling funds
- Almost all VC funds as an LP
- Most private real estate, hedge funds, etc.

What you *can* do unaccredited:
- **Reg CF platforms** (Republic, Wefunder, StartEngine) — equity crowdfunding, open to everyone, capped at how much you can invest per year. Adverse selection problem I mentioned earlier — strong founders rarely raise this way.
- **Public markets** — stocks, ETFs, public REITs.
- **Build the paper-trading muscle** — costs nothing, transfers directly when you do qualify.

**The realistic path for you**

The Series 65 is interesting and underrated. It's a self-study exam (~100–150 hours of prep), costs ~$200, and once passed and registered (you'd need to affiliate with a state-registered investment advisor or register as one yourself, which has its own friction), you're accredited regardless of net worth. Some people pass it purely to unlock accredited status. Worth knowing it exists.

Otherwise, the income path is probably the cleanest as your SkyWest captain track + OSA revenue compounds. Two consecutive $200K+ years and you're in.

The Series 65 route — want me to go deeper on what that actually involves? It's the only one of these that's a decision you could act on this quarter rather than wait on.

## Phelipe

What a typical angel investor returns on 5 years

---

## Claude

Short answer: **most angel investors lose money or barely break even at 5 years.** The asset class only starts looking good at 7–10 years, and only for angels with enough portfolio breadth for the power law to work. Let me search for the most current data so I'm not quoting stale numbers.
```
This block is not supported on your current device yet.
```

Here's what the research actually says — and the headline numbers hide the real story.

**The "official" answer**

Industry studies (Wiltbank, Angel Capital Association) put diversified angel portfolios at an internal rate of return between 22% and 27% across nearly three decades of data, with the original Wiltbank study finding an average return of 2.6x the investment over 3.5 years, or roughly 27% IRR. Sounds incredible. Better than the S&P. Better than VC funds.

**The reality the headline hides**

That 27% number is *survivor bias on steroids*. It comes from organized angel groups who do heavy diligence, write 25+ checks, and self-report. AngelList ran a much broader study on 10,665 actual investor portfolios and got a very different picture: the realized and unrealized IRR for all investments was 15%, investors with 1–5 investments had a median return of 0.0% IRR, and the top 10% of investors earned 50% of the returns.

The portfolio-size effect is brutal: 10-company portfolios had a median IRR of about 6% with 32% of investors losing money; 20-company portfolios had a median IRR of about 7% with 16% losing money; 50-company portfolios had a median IRR of about 10% with 11% losing money.

Translation: **if you write 5 checks, your expected return is roughly zero.** You need 20+ to even get into mediocre-positive-IRR territory, and 50+ to start looking respectable.

**The 5-year question specifically**

This is the part most people don't internalize. For angel funds launched in 2021, the median IRR was 1.2% — and actual cash distributed back was less than 2% of investments 75% of the time. Five years in, you've gotten basically nothing in cash. The "returns" are paper markups on companies that haven't exited.

Compare to 2017-vintage funds, which had a median IRR of 19.7% — but cash distributed was less than 64% of invested capital 75% of the time. Even at year 8, most investors hadn't gotten their money back in cash, despite "good" IRRs.

**The honest synthesis**

At the 5-year mark for a typical angel:

- **Cash in hand:** usually less than what you put in. Exits take 7–10 years.
- **Paper IRR:** wildly variable. If you wrote 5 checks: probably 0% or negative. If you wrote 25+ in a decent vintage: maybe 10–20% on paper.
- **Realized IRR:** much lower than paper. Markdowns happen, "winners" die at Series B, and the power-law winners that drive returns usually exit at year 8–12.

The studies showing 22–27% are real but describe a specific population: experienced angels in groups, with large diversified portfolios, doing 20–40 hours of diligence per deal, and active in their portfolio companies. That's not a hobby — it's a part-time job.

**For your situation:** if you're "exploring," the most useful number isn't the headline IRR. It's that **a 5-check angel has a median return of 0% and a 65% chance of underperforming a Vanguard index fund**, which compounded at ~12% over the same period with zero work. The asset class only beats public markets if you commit to it as a real practice.

This is exactly why I pushed paper-trading earlier — building the judgment muscle first is free, and tells you if you actually have an edge before you find out the expensive way.

## Phelipe

How to become a good angel that will lead to starting a vc?

---

## Claude

Now we're talking about the real game. This is a different question than "how do I angel invest" — you're asking about the **angel-to-GP path**, which has a specific shape and a brutal filter.

Let me give you the honest map.

**The track record problem**

To raise a fund, you need a story LPs can underwrite. There are exactly three credible origin stories for a first-time GP:

1. **The operator** — you built and sold a company, now you invest.
2. **The track record** — you angeled into 3+ companies that became unicorns or large exits, with verifiable check sizes and entry valuations.
3. **The brand/network** — you're a content/community force whose dealflow is structurally better than incumbents (think Packy McCormick, Harry Stebbings).

Almost all first-time funds raise on some combination of these three. "Smart person who likes startups" is not on the list. LPs hear that pitch 50 times a week.

**The track record path specifically (the one your question implies)**

Here's the bar, roughly: to raise even a small first fund ($5–15M), you generally need **3–5 angel investments with marked-up valuations, ideally 1–2 with 10x+ paper markups, and at least one realized exit or near-exit**. This typically takes 5–8 years from your first check, because companies need time to mark up.

The math working against you: you need to write enough checks to get hits (power law says 20+), you need check sizes large enough that LPs take you seriously ($10–25K minimum, ideally $25–50K), and you need to lead or co-lead some rounds so you have signal as a *picker*, not just a passenger on someone else's deal. Riding 50 syndicates teaches you nothing LPs care about — they want to see you sourced and decided.

That's roughly **$500K–$1.5M of personal capital deployed over 5–7 years before you have a fundable track record.** This is why most angels-to-GPs were either already wealthy operators or had wealthy backers writing alongside them as a "syndicate."

**The faster path: become indispensable to a fund first**

The realistic shortcut isn't "angel for 7 years then raise" — it's **become a scout, then a venture partner, then a GP at an existing fund, then spin out**. Sequence:

1. **Scout for an established fund** (Sequoia Scouts, Lightspeed Scouts, a16z scout program, plus dozens of smaller ones). They give you $25–100K to deploy, share carry, and you get reps without risking your own capital. Invitation-only, but the entry ticket is *being known for unique dealflow in a domain*. For you, that domain is aviation/aerospace + AI-native vertical platforms. There are maybe 30 people in the country who can credibly source aviation deals. You can be one of them in 18 months if OSA is the platform you make it.

2. **Venture Partner / Operator-in-Residence** at a small fund — part-time, deal-flow-and-diligence focused, sometimes carry-bearing. This is where you learn the *job* of investing (term sheets, board dynamics, follow-on math, LP relations).

3. **Principal or Partner at an existing fund** — the actual GP apprenticeship.

4. **Spin out** with track record and LP relationships from steps 1–3.

This is how almost every <40-year-old GP I can think of got there. The "I angeled my way into a fund from nothing" story exists but is rare and usually has hidden capital behind it.

**What you specifically should do, given OSA**

Your situation is unusual in a useful way. You're building a vertical AI-native platform in an industry (private aviation) where almost no early-stage investors have domain expertise. That's not a side detail — it's the entire wedge.

Concrete moves, in order:

1. **Build OSA into a platform people want to be near.** Founders, scouts, and LPs all chase access. If OSA becomes the place where aviation-tech founders, sustainable aviation fuel startups, eVTOL companies, MRO software, and adjacent dealflow naturally cluster — you become the person every generalist fund calls when they see an aviation deal. That's pre-fund LP relationship building, except it doesn't look like it.

2. **Write publicly about aviation-tech investing**, not just OSA. Loud Hawk is already positioned for this. A "what I'd fund in aviation" thesis post quarterly does more for future GP credibility than 20 small angel checks. Brad Gerstner, Packy, Gavin Baker, Doug O'Laughlin — they all built investor reputations through public thinking before deploying meaningful capital.

3. **Once accredited, scout before you angel.** Pitch Sequoia Scouts, South Park Commons, Inflection, niche aerospace funds (Boost VC, Embedded Ventures, Marlinspike). You bring rare dealflow; they give you a checkbook and credentialing. Better risk-adjusted than your own checks.

4. **Make 5–10 highly concentrated angel bets in aviation/aero-adjacent companies over years 3–5.** Not a diversified portfolio. A *thesis-driven* one. LPs care more about "you saw this aviation deal three years before anyone and led it" than "you have 30 generic SaaS angels." Concentration with thesis is more fundable than diversification without.

5. **Year 5–7: SPV-led syndicates.** Lead a couple of deals through your own SPV with friends and family + scout-fund LPs co-investing. This is the dress rehearsal for fund-raising. You learn capital formation, GP-LP dynamics, deal structuring — at small scale, with stakes.

6. **Year 7–10: raise Fund I.** Probably $10–30M. Aviation-tech and adjacent vertical AI thesis. By then you have OSA as the reputational asset, 2–3 marked-up scout/angel deals, an SPV track record, and an LP base that already co-invested with you informally.

**The uncomfortable truth**

The path is 7–10 years minimum, costs hundreds of thousands of dollars in personal capital even if you do it well, and the survivor rate of first-time funds is grim — most GPs never raise a Fund II. You're betting a decade of your career and meaningful capital on judgment that most people don't actually have, and you can't know if you have it until you've deployed enough to find out.

But there's a version of this that's specifically right for you, and it routes *through* OSA rather than around it. **OSA is your first fund.** You're already running portfolio construction, capital allocation, market making, founder-style picking — just inside one company instead of across many. If OSA works, the GP path opens naturally because operator-with-exit beats track-record-only every time. If OSA doesn't work, the track-record-only path is uphill.

So the honest answer to "how do I become a good angel that leads to starting a VC" is: **make OSA work, build public domain authority, scout instead of angeling early, then concentrate your eventual angel checks where your edge is real.** Don't try to run two paths in parallel — your edge in both is the same thing.

Want to go deeper on the scout program landscape, or on what an aviation-tech investment thesis would actually look like as a public artifact?

## Claude

Now we're modeling the actual game. Let me run this honestly — no inflated numbers.

**Capital math, 10 years**

$50–70K/year × 10 years = **$500K–$700K deployed**. Call it $600K base case.

That's enough capital to matter. Most "angels" never deploy this much. Two things to decide upfront:

- **Check size strategy:** $600K across 30 deals = $20K avg checks. That's the right zone — large enough that founders take you seriously and you can lead small rounds, small enough to get reps. Going below $10K average is a mistake; you're invisible on the cap table and can't lead. Going above $40K means too few reps to learn from.
- **Pacing:** ~3 deals/year for 10 years. That's a real pace. Sequoia Scouts deploy at roughly that rate. It also lets you spread across vintages so you're not all-in on one market cycle.

**Realistic outcomes at year 10, $600K deployed across ~30 thesis-concentrated deals in aerospace/aviation/deep tech**

Three scenarios, weighted by what the data actually shows:

**Bear case (~40% probability)**: 1.0–1.5x cash-on-cash. ~5% IRR. You get back $600K–$900K total over 10 years with most of it returning in years 8–12. Roughly 22–25 of 30 companies go to zero or limp. You have 1–2 modest exits (3–5x). No grand slam. You underperformed the S&P. **You don't raise a fund.**

**Base case (~45% probability)**: 2–3x cash-on-cash. 12–18% IRR. $1.2–1.8M total returns. Around 25 of 30 companies are zero or near-zero, 3–4 are modest winners (3–10x), and you hit one ~20–30x outcome. This is the realistic "good but not great" angel outcome. **You're fundable for a small Fund I, but it's tight — LPs want to see at least one breakout marked at 50x+ paper.**

**Bull case (~15% probability)**: 5–10x+ cash-on-cash. 25–35% IRR. $3M–$6M+ returns. You hit one 100x outcome (the company you saw early because of your aviation domain edge — eVTOL, sustainable aviation fuel, defense aerospace, vertical AI in aviation). **This is the fundable outcome. You raise Fund I at $20–40M with conviction LPs.**

The remaining ~5% is the catastrophic case — bad vintages, bad picks, bad luck — where you return well under 1x.

**What the data says about your specific setup**

Your portfolio of ~30 deals puts you in a much better statistical place than typical hobbyist angels. From the numbers we pulled earlier: 50-company portfolios had median IRR ~10% with 11% of investors losing money; 20-company had median ~7% with 16% losing money. You'll be between those — **median expected outcome around 8–12% IRR**, comparable to or slightly below S&P, with much higher variance.

Translation: **you are mathematically *more likely* to have underperformed a Vanguard index than to have beaten it**, if all you do is the angel checks themselves.

**But here's what changes the math for you specifically**

You have three structural advantages most angels don't:

1. **Domain edge in aerospace/aviation.** Generalist angels in deep tech are flying blind on technical risk; you can read an aviation cap table, certification timeline, and operator economics in ways that compress diligence from weeks to hours. This is the #1 driver of returns in the Wiltbank data (sector expertise + 20–40 hrs diligence per deal).

2. **OSA as a reputational moat.** If OSA becomes recognizable in aviation-tech, your dealflow for the *next* aviation startup founder will be top-3-calls level. You'll see deals before they're competitive. This is the only sustainable angel edge.

3. **Loud Hawk + content.** Public thinking → inbound dealflow → better picks → better track record → fundable.

If you compound those three, you're not in the "median angel" bucket anymore. You're in the bucket where domain-specialist angels get to ~25% IRR because they actually know what they're looking at.

**Realistic 10-year outcome with all three advantages firing:**

- **Capital deployed:** $600K
- **Year 10 paper portfolio value:** $1.5–4M (base to bull)
- **Realized cash by year 10:** $400K–$1.5M (most exits cluster in years 8–14)
- **Track record:** 2–4 marked-up companies in aerospace/aviation, ideally 1 with 30x+ paper, hopefully 1 small realized exit
- **Reputational asset:** Loud Hawk publication, OSA as platform, recognized as the aviation-tech voice in venture
- **Fund-raisability:** if base case or better — yes, $15–30M Fund I is plausible at year 10–11

**The unsexy honest version**

Most likely outcome: you deploy $600K, have ~$1.5M in paper value at year 10 (mostly illiquid), have realized $300–500K in cash, and have a track record that's *fundable but not impressive*. You raise a small first fund — maybe $10–15M — heavily backed by people who know you personally and an anchor LP who believes the OSA story. You spend years 11–14 proving Fund I, raise Fund II at $30–50M.

The bull case is real but it's 15%, not 50%.

**The arbitrage I'd push you toward**

Given your numbers and time horizon, consider this allocation for the $50–70K/year:

- **$30–40K/year → angel checks** (15–20 deals over 10 years, concentrated in aerospace/aviation/deep tech)
- **$10–15K/year → scout-fund LP positions or small VC rolling funds** (vintage diversification, see how pros pick, build LP relationships)
- **$10K/year → public thinking + reputational capital** (Loud Hawk production, conferences, content production for HAWK)

That's still $500–650K over 10 years going productively at the GP path, just split across vehicles. Pure angel checks is the highest-variance route. The blended route gets you to "fundable" with more reliability.

**The bigger question I'd flag**

The opportunity cost question matters more than the angel return question. **The single highest-EV use of $600K over 10 years for someone with your specific setup is probably... OSA.** Not angel checks. If OSA Connect does $5M GMV in year 3, $50M in year 5, $500M in year 10, your equity is worth orders of magnitude more than any reasonable angel portfolio outcome. And the GP path opens easier from "founder of OSA" than from "angel with 2x portfolio."

So the meta-answer: **fund OSA first, angel second.** Once OSA has reached the point where additional capital from you doesn't accelerate it (probably post-Series A, when institutional capital is doing the work), redirect the surplus into the angel program. Until then, every dollar to angels is a dollar not compounding inside the asset that has the highest expected return in your life.

Want to model what "angel program funded by OSA exit/dividends" looks like instead, or break down the scout program landscape so you can start positioning for those now without writing checks?

**The deployable-to-angel question**

Here's where it gets real. EBITDA isn't take-home. You have to walk it through:

- **Federal corp tax** — Delaware C-Corp, 21% federal flat. State varies (Illinois adds ~9.5% combined effective on apportioned income). Effective combined ~25–28%.
- **Distributions to you as founder/sole shareholder** — taxed again as qualified dividends at 23.8% (20% LTCG + 3.8% NIIT) since you'll be in the top bracket.

So the double-tax math on a C-Corp distribution is brutal: **$1 of EBITDA → ~$0.55–0.60 in your personal account.**

But you have levers. The smart bootstrap playbook:

1. **Reasonable W-2 salary** to yourself (~$250–400K) — deductible to corp, taxed as ordinary income to you. Single layer of tax.
2. **Reinvest the rest** in OSA growth, R&D credits, retained earnings — no current tax bite.
3. **Distribute strategically** only what you actually need to deploy outside OSA.

Realistic personal cash available for angel deployment, after living expenses and OSA reinvestment:

| Year | EBITDA | Reinvest in OSA | Your W-2 + Distributions (net) | Angel Deployable |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3 | $1.75M | ~$700K | ~$600K take-home | ~$200–300K |
| 5 | $2.5M | ~$1.0M | ~$800K take-home | ~$400–500K |
| 7 | $3.6M | ~$1.2M | ~$1.1M take-home | ~$700–900K |
| 10 | $6.2M | ~$1.8M | ~$1.6M take-home | ~$1.2–1.5M |

These assume you keep household expenses reasonable and don't lifestyle-creep, and that you actually do reinvest meaningfully in OSA (CTO comp, ops hires, infrastructure, working capital for charter prepayments).

**Total angel capital deployable, years 3–10**

Roughly **$4–6M over 8 years**, ramping from $200K/year early to $1.2M+/year late.

That changes the angel math fundamentally compared to the $50–70K/year scenario.

**What this size of program actually buys you**

At $4–6M deployed:

- **Check sizes can scale up.** Years 3–4: $25K average. Years 7–10: $75–150K average, with ability to lead seed rounds and take 5–10% ownership in companies you have real conviction on.
- **Portfolio shape:** ~40–60 companies over 8 years, but heavily concentrated in the back half. Or alternative: ~25 companies with much larger checks and lead positions, which is more fundable as a GP track record.
- **You can lead.** Leading a seed round (writing the largest check, setting the price, taking the board seat) is fundamentally different from following. LPs evaluating you as a future GP weight lead checks 5–10x more than follower checks.
- **You can do follow-ons.** Reserve 30–40% of capital for follow-on into your top 3–5 winners. This is where most amateur angels miss returns — they do their seed check, then get diluted to nothing because they can't write the Series A pro-rata.

**Realistic 10-year outcome with this capital base**

You're no longer in "median angel" territory. You're in **emerging-manager-with-balance-sheet** territory, which is a structurally different game.

- **Year 10 paper portfolio:** $10–25M base case, $40M+ bull
- **Realized cash by year 10:** $2–6M (still mostly illiquid; angel timeline)
- **Track record:** 3–6 marked-up companies, ideally 1–2 led rounds at 30x+ paper, possibly 1 unicorn outcome
- **Reputation:** OSA is an industry-recognized aviation platform doing $12M+ revenue, you've been publishing on Loud Hawk for 7+ years, you've led aerospace deals other VCs co-invested in

**The fund-raising implication**

At year 10 in this scenario, you don't need to raise a fund the way most first-time GPs do. You have three options:

1. **Raise traditional Fund I** ($30–75M) — much easier than the lean scenario. You're an operator with a real exit story (OSA EBITDA + revenue), a 10-year angel track record with led deals, and an industry brand. Top-decile first-time GP profile.

2. **Self-funded venture studio** — you keep deploying $1–2M/year of OSA EBITDA permanently as a "single-LP fund" with no carry obligations, no fund cycle, no LP politics. Pat Grady-style or like Marc Andreessen pre-a16z. Lower scale, infinite optionality.

3. **Hybrid: Fund I + OSA balance sheet** — raise a $40M Fund I but also keep deploying separately from OSA. This is what Naval did with MetaStable + AngelList angel checks in parallel.

**The strategic answer to your question**

You can deploy roughly **$300K–$1.5M/year as angel capital**, ramping over years 3–10, totaling $4–6M, *if* OSA hits the trajectory you described. That's enough capital to run a serious thesis-driven angel program — not a hobby, not a typical angel, but a credible emerging investor with a real balance sheet behind them.

The ratio I'd push: **deploy 25–35% of post-tax personal cash into angel, not more**. The rest stays liquid (Treasuries, public markets) for personal optionality and OSA contingency. Angel illiquidity is brutal — if OSA hits a rough year and you've sunk 80% of your liquid net worth into 8-year-locked private positions, you're forced to sell low or take dilutive personal capital.

**The thing I'd flag**

The bootstrap-OSA-then-angel path is the highest-EV path I've modeled, *but* it has one structural risk: 50% EBITDA on a charter brokerage is aggressive. Industry comps run 15–30% EBITDA at scale because of working capital needs (you often prepay operators before client funds clear), bad debt, broker comp, and tech investment. OSA Aircraft sales might support 50%+ on the transaction fee side. A blended 50% is achievable but assumes you stay disciplined on overhead and Aircraft becomes a meaningful share of revenue.

If real EBITDA lands at 30%, divide all the angel-deployable numbers by ~1.6. Still a real program, just smaller.

## Phelipe

So the chances of survival increase with more deal? A self funded venture studio (angel investor). What is the winning size ?

## Claude

Two questions, both important. Let me answer them in order because the second depends on the first.

**Yes — survival scales with portfolio size, but with diminishing returns**

The data is unambiguous on this. From the AngelList study we pulled earlier:

- 5 deals → median 0% IRR, ~50%+ chance of underperforming a savings account
- 10 deals → median ~6% IRR, 32% of investors lose money
- 20 deals → median ~7% IRR, 16% lose money
- 50 deals → median ~10% IRR, 11% lose money
- 100+ deals → median ~12–15% IRR (extrapolated), <10% lose money

Two things to notice:

1. **The "stop losing money" curve flattens fast.** Going from 5 → 30 deals collapses your downside risk dramatically. Going from 30 → 100 helps less.
2. **The median IRR curve is shallow.** Even at 100 deals, median is ~12–15%. The math reveals what's really happening: more deals doesn't make your *picks* better — it just gives the **power law more chances to fire**. You need enough at-bats for the 1-in-30 monster to land in your portfolio.

**The mechanical reason**

Roughly 1 in 30 angel-stage investments returns 30x+. If you write 5 checks, your probability of catching one is ~16%. Write 30, it's ~64%. Write 60, it's ~87%. Write 100, it's ~97%.

**But here's the catch:** even if you catch one 30x, your portfolio IRR depends on whether you wrote a meaningful check into it. If your 30x company got a $5K check from you while your $50K checks went to the duds, the math doesn't save you. This is why **portfolio construction matters more than portfolio size past a certain point.**

The real winning shape isn't "as many deals as possible." It's:

- Enough deals to catch the power law (30+ minimum)
- Concentrated enough that you actually own meaningful positions in winners (not $1K spray-and-pray)
- Reserved capital for follow-ons in winners (this is where 50%+ of returns come from for top-decile angels)

**Now: the venture studio / self-funded fund question**

This is a different vehicle than angel investing, and the sizing logic is completely different.

A self-funded venture studio (sometimes called a **family office venture arm** or **single-LP fund**) is just you, deploying your own balance sheet, with no outside LPs, no fund cycle, no carry obligations. You ARE the LP and the GP. The "winning size" depends on what game you're trying to play.

Three credible scale points:

**Tier 1: Serious angel ($1–3M annual deployment, $10–30M total over 10 years)**

This is what your post-OSA-success model gets you to. Run as a venture studio (named entity, public thesis, dedicated process), it's enough to:

- Lead 3–5 rounds per year at $250–500K checks
- Reserve 40% for follow-ons
- Concentrate in your domain edge (aviation/aero/deep tech)
- Build a real reputation as a domain investor

This is roughly the scale of **Lachy Groom's** early activity, **Elad Gil's** original angel program, **Naval Ravikant's MetaStable-era**, or **Balaji Srinivasan's** deployment before he had institutional vehicles. Important people, real returns, no fund mechanics.

**Tier 2: Operator-fund ($5–15M annual deployment, $50–150M total)**

This is the **Pioneer Fund / South Park Commons / Susa Ventures** scale. At this size, you're effectively running a small fund using your own capital. You can:

- Lead Series A rounds, not just seed
- Take board seats at companies you lead
- Hire 1–2 investing partners
- Compete with traditional small funds for deals

This requires either a major OSA exit ($50–100M+ to your personal balance sheet) or a partial OSA secondary plus continued operating cashflow. Not your year-10 base case. Plausible at year 12–15 if OSA continues compounding or has a recap event.

**Tier 3: Permanent capital firm ($25M+ annual, $250M+ total)**

This is **Thrive Capital** when Joshua Kushner started, **Founders Fund** at the Thiel level, **Iconiq** for Zuck. Requires generational wealth from a major exit. Out of scope for any reasonable OSA outcome unless OSA becomes a $1B+ company.

**The "winning size" answer for you specifically**

For someone in your position with the OSA bootstrap trajectory you outlined, the winning size is **Tier 1 — $1–2M/year deployment, $10–20M total over 10 years**, structured as a named venture studio.

Why this is the right size, not bigger:

1. **It matches your realistic capital base.** You said 25–35% of post-tax personal cash. From the EBITDA model, that's ~$800K–$1.5M/year mid-stream, scaling to $1.5M+ later.

2. **It's enough to play the real game.** You can lead seed rounds (~$500K leads at $5–10M post are common in 2025 seed). You can take pro-rata. You can build the led-deal track record that makes you fundable later if you want to scale to outside LPs.

3. **It's small enough that focus matters.** You'll do 4–8 deals per year, each with 20+ hours of diligence. This is the band where the Wiltbank data shows 25%+ IRR for sector specialists. Going bigger means more deals → less attention → returns regress to median.

4. **Domain concentration works at this scale.** Aviation/aerospace/deep tech doesn't have 50 fundable deals per year. There are maybe 15–25 worth chasing. Tier 1 lets you fund the right 5–7. Tier 2 forces you to stretch outside your edge to deploy capital.

**The portfolio shape that wins at Tier 1**

Over 10 years of $1–2M annual deployment:

- **35–50 total companies** (right zone for power law to work)
- **Average check $250–400K with significant variance** — $500K+ leads, $100K passive co-invests
- **40% reserve ratio** for follow-ons in top 5 companies
- **Domain concentration:** 60%+ in aerospace/aviation/deep tech, 40% opportunistic adjacencies
- **Lead vs. follow:** Aim for 30%+ of capital in led deals (this is the GP-track-record bar)

**The honest synthesis**

The mathematical answer to "winning size" is whatever portfolio is large enough to catch power-law winners (30+ companies) and concentrated enough that your meaningful checks land in those winners (not spray-and-pray).

The strategic answer for you specifically is that **the venture studio you can self-fund from OSA EBITDA is roughly the same size as a small institutional Fund I, except you keep 100% of the carry and don't have LP obligations**. That's a structurally better deal than raising a fund, *if* OSA can sustain the EBITDA — and it gives you the optional path to convert into a traditional GP later if you want to scale.

You don't need to be a billionaire to run a credible permanent-capital venture program. You need ~$10–20M of deployable capital over a decade, deep domain edge, public reputation, and the patience to wait 7–10 years for outcomes.

Your model gets you there. The risk is execution on OSA, not the angel thesis.

Want to dig into how the Tier 1 venture studio gets *named, structured, and positioned* (entity, brand, public thesis)? That's the next question if this is where you're heading.

## Claude

Good question. Let me model this concretely for the Tier 1 venture studio path — $10–20M deployed over 10 years across ~40 companies in aerospace/aviation/deep tech.

I'll build three scenarios with the actual portfolio mechanics, not just headline IRR numbers.

---

**Setup assumptions (all three cases)**

- **Total deployed:** $15M over 10 years
- **Companies:** 40
- **Average initial check:** $250K
- **Follow-on reserves:** 40% of capital ($6M) deployed into top 5–7 winners
- **Hold period to exit:** 7–10 years per company
- **Exit window:** Years 7–15 from first check (so cash returns mostly in years 10–18)

Power-law distribution holds across all cases. What changes is the *quality of your top 1–3 outcomes*, which is where 80%+ of returns come from.

---

**Bear case (~30% probability)**

The portfolio works, but no monsters.

- **Outcome distribution:** 28 zeros, 8 modest exits (2–4x), 3 decent exits (8–15x), 1 strong exit (25x), 0 grand slams
- **Best deal:** A defense aerospace company you led at $8M post, exits at $200M. Your $500K (initial + follow-on) returns ~$12M
- **Decent winners:** Three aviation-software companies, modest acquisitions
- **Total returns:** ~$30–40M
- **Multiple on invested capital (MOIC):** 2.0–2.6x
- **IRR:** 8–12%

You've underperformed the S&P over the same period (which compounds at ~11% historically). The angel program was a wash.

**You don't raise a Fund I.** Your track record is "fine" but not exciting. LPs see one ~25x deal and think "lucky." You either keep going with personal capital or stop.

---

**Base case (~50% probability)**

This is what a competent thesis-driven domain specialist actually achieves.

- **Outcome distribution:** 25 zeros, 9 modest exits (2–4x), 4 strong (10–20x), 1 breakout (50x), 1 outlier (100x+)
- **Breakout #1:** An eVTOL company you led at $15M post-seed. Series A-D rounds dilute you, but your ~$1M deployed (initial + follow-on) is worth $50M at exit/IPO at $3B valuation. Returns ~$50M
- **Outlier:** A vertical AI/aerospace SaaS you backed at seed for $300K. Sold to a strategic for $1.2B. After dilution, your stake returns ~$30M
- **Total returns:** ~$110–140M
- **MOIC:** 7–9x
- **IRR:** 22–28%

This is **clearly fund-raisable**. You have two top-decile outcomes, both led or co-led, in your domain of expertise, with a coherent thesis behind them. You can raise a $40–75M Fund I at year 10–11 with strong LP demand.

You also have ~$30–50M of realized cash by year 10–12 (with more to come), which means **you can be the anchor LP in your own Fund I**. That's the strongest possible signal to other LPs.

---

**Bull case (~15% probability)**

Power law fires hard. One of your concentrated bets becomes a generational outcome.

- **Outcome distribution:** Similar to base, but one deal becomes a $5–20B+ outcome
- **The monster:** You led the seed round of a defense/space/AI company in 2027 that becomes the next Anduril or Palantir. Your $750K (initial + multiple pro-rata follow-ons) is worth $200–400M at IPO/exit
- **Other winners:** Base case stack still applies underneath
- **Total returns:** $250–500M+
- **MOIC:** 17–35x
- **IRR:** 35–45%+

You're now in **Naval / Elad Gil / Lachy Groom territory.** You don't need to raise a fund — outside LPs are begging to give you money. You have the option to scale into a $200M+ fund whenever you want, or stay solo and become a generational angel.

This is the outcome where the venture studio path *dramatically* outperforms running a traditional fund, because you kept 100% of carry on a $300M return.

---

**The disaster case (~5% probability)**

For honesty's sake, this exists too.

- Bad vintage timing (you deploy heavily in 2027–28 right before a deep tech correction)
- No power-law winners — your two best outcomes are 5–10x
- 32 of 40 companies zero out (worse than typical because of vintage)
- **Total returns:** ~$10–15M on $15M deployed
- **MOIC:** 0.7–1.0x
- **IRR:** -2% to +1%

You lost real money in inflation-adjusted terms. Capital is locked up for years. The angel thesis didn't work for you. You stop, redeploy remaining liquid capital into public markets, and OSA carries the family financial future alone.

---

**Probability-weighted expected outcome**

Running the math:

| Case | Probability | MOIC | $ Returns |
|---|---|---|---|
| Disaster | 5% | 0.85x | $13M |
| Bear | 30% | 2.3x | $35M |
| Base | 50% | 8x | $120M |
| Bull | 15% | 25x | $375M |

**Expected return:** ~$120M on $15M deployed = ~8x MOIC, ~22% IRR

That's the headline number, but the **median outcome** (50th percentile) is the base case at ~7–9x. The expected value is pulled up by the 15% bull case.

---

**The thing the numbers hide**

Two important caveats:

1. **Liquidity timing is brutal.** Even in the base case, you might have $30M of paper value at year 10 but only $5–8M of *cash*. Most exits cluster years 10–15. Plan accordingly — don't deploy money you'll need before year 12.

2. **Your domain edge changes the distribution.** All these probabilities assume you're a *competent thesis-driven specialist*. Generic angels do worse. The 50% base case probability is achievable specifically because aviation/aerospace/deep tech has fewer informed investors than consumer or generic SaaS, so your edge compounds. If you tried to run this same program in B2B SaaS, the bear/disaster probabilities would roughly double and the bull case would halve.

---

**The honest punchline**

The Tier 1 venture studio path, executed competently from your OSA bootstrap base, has roughly:

- **~80% chance of returning your capital plus inflation**
- **~65% chance of beating the S&P over the same period**
- **~50% chance of achieving 5x+ MOIC and being clearly fund-raisable**
- **~15% chance of generational returns**
- **~5% chance of losing meaningful capital**

That's a much better risk profile than "the median angel," who has a coin-flip chance of underperforming Treasuries. Your edge — domain knowledge + OSA platform + public thinking + concentrated portfolio — is what shifts the distribution.

**The strategic question isn't "what return can I expect."** It's "is the *expected* base case ($30–50M of realized cash + $20–40M of paper value at year 12, plus a fundable track record, plus optionality on a $50M+ Fund I) worth the opportunity cost vs. simply reinvesting that $15M into OSA itself or public markets?"

That's a real question worth modeling next if you want to go there. Want to compare the three vehicles side by side — angel program vs. OSA reinvestment vs. public market index?

Good — I have what I need. Here are the actual numbers, with the important caveats most articles bury.

---

**Y Combinator — the headline vs. the reality**

The flashy number: YC has invested in over 5,668 companies as of 2026 with a combined valuation of $600 billion. That's the "portfolio value" headline you see everywhere.

But that number is mostly **paper, not cash**, and it's heavily concentrated. The top four YC companies — Airbnb, DoorDash, Coinbase, and Instacart — account for over 84% of total public market value created by YC alumni, representing more than $200 billion in market capitalization.

What YC actually invested vs. what it might be worth: YC invested roughly $1 billion across 5,000 companies, which have grown to a combined valuation of $600 billion. So on a paper basis, that's roughly a 600x return on capital invested — but YC owns ~7% on average, gets diluted heavily through subsequent rounds, and most of the $600B is unrealized.

The realistic estimate of YC's **realized** cash returns since 2005 is probably in the **$8–15B range** (Airbnb stake alone was likely worth $5B+ at IPO; Coinbase, DoorDash, Dropbox, Instacart added several billion more). Still extraordinary for ~$1B invested over 20 years, but ~10x of headline AUM, not 600x.

The skill curve, even at YC: roughly 4.5% of YC companies become unicorns vs. 2.5% for other venture-backed seed-stage startups, and around 45% of YC companies raise a Series A vs. the 33% average. Better than average — not magic.

---

**Andreessen Horowitz (a16z) — the bigger machine**

Scale: As of March 2026, the firm is managing at least $90 billion in assets. In January 2026, Andreessen Horowitz raised $15 billion across five new funds.

Realized returns to LPs: Andreessen Horowitz has returned at least $25 billion net to its backers since the firm's founding in 2009. That's the actual cash figure, not paper TVPI.

The legendary fund: AH III sits at an 11.3x Net TVPI (total value to paid-in capital after fees) as of September 30 2025, and when you include the parallel fund, it's at a 9.1x Net TVPI. AH III includes Coinbase, which resulted in distributions of $7 billion gross to a16z LPs across the funds it's in, Databricks, Pinterest, GitHub, and Lyft, and is one of the best performing large venture funds of all time.

A 9–11x net TVPI on a $1.5B fund is roughly $13–17B of value created off that single vintage. That's *one* fund out of dozens.

For context on how this happens: on a $1.6 billion AI Infrastructure fund, if they own 8% of a company at exit, they need a $20 billion exit to return the fund — and Andreessen seems to have quite a few of them.

---

**The comparison that actually matters for your question**

| | YC | a16z |
|---|---|---|
| Founded | 2005 | 2009 |
| Total deployed | ~$1B | ~$25–30B |
| AUM/Portfolio value | $600B portfolio | $90B AUM |
| Estimated realized to LPs | $8–15B | $25B+ |
| Top-fund TVPI | Not public | 9–11x (AH III) |
| Approach | Volume + selection | Concentration + platform |

---

**What this tells you for the OSA-to-VC path**

Three uncomfortable truths worth absorbing:

**1. The "$600B portfolio" framing is misleading and you'll see it everywhere.** It's the number marketed to founders and LPs, but it conflates paper markups with cash returns. When you see a fund advertise "$X billion in portfolio value," mentally divide by 5–10x to get realistic realized returns. This matters because LPs evaluating *your* future fund will look past TVPI to DPI (distributions to paid-in) — actual cash returned. Don't build your career on inflated paper.

**2. YC's edge is volume + brand. a16z's edge is platform + concentration.** Two completely different games. YC funds 700+ companies a year and lets the power law sort it out. a16z makes 30–50 concentrated bets and amplifies them with a platform team larger than most VC firms. **Your aviation venture studio path is closer to a16z's model than YC's** — concentrated thesis, deep domain expertise, platform leverage (in your case, OSA itself as the platform). You can't out-volume YC. You can out-domain everyone in aerospace.

**3. The path-dependency is brutal.** AH III hit because Coinbase. YC hit because Airbnb + Stripe + DoorDash + Coinbase. Without those specific outcomes, both firms look mid-tier. **You need 1–2 generational outcomes in your portfolio over 10–15 years. Period.** That's the entire game. Everything else — reputation, dealflow, operational support — is in service of finding those 1–2 deals or being trusted enough by the founder to get into them.

For aerospace, the candidates over the next decade are roughly: a defense prime (Anduril-class) you back early, an eVTOL winner (Joby/Archer/something newer), a hypersonics company, an autonomous aviation/UAS leader, a sustainable aviation fuel breakout, or an AI-aviation infrastructure layer. **You probably need to be in 1 of those 6 to fund-raise convincingly.**

Your edge: as an active commercial pilot building an aviation platform, you'll see those founders before generalist VCs do. That's a real moat, not a story.

The honest punchline on the YC/a16z question: **they made what they made because they were in the room when the generational outcomes happened.** They didn't pick better than everyone else 95% of the time — they had access to the deals that mattered when those deals mattered. Your version of "being in the room" runs through OSA being recognized as *the* aviation-tech platform. That's why the platform thesis matters more than the angel thesis. The angel checks are downstream of platform credibility.

## Phelipe

How a16z has so many funds?

---

## Claude

Good question — and the answer is the most important strategic shift in venture in the last 15 years. a16z basically rewrote the playbook.

**The old VC model (pre-2009)**

One firm = one fund at a time, raised every 3–4 years, ~$200–500M in size, generalist tech focus, 3–5 partners, all investing out of the same pool. Every LP got the same exposure to every deal. Sequoia, Benchmark, Kleiner — they all worked this way for decades.

The constraint was simple: a single fund can only get so big before the math breaks. When Marc and I started the firm in 2009, the conventional wisdom in Venture Capital was that in any given year, only 15 companies would ever generate $100M in revenue and those 15 companies would drive almost all of VC returns. Venture Capital firms configured themselves to address a market of 15 important companies. This meant relatively small fund sizes and a small number of partners in a single fund was the optimal approach. Andreessen Horowitz began in exactly this way with a $300M fund and 2 General Partners.

**What changed**

Shortly after we started the firm, all that began to change. My partner Marc saw the new world coming and wrote about it in 2011 in a piece called "Software is Eating the World." What he predicted then came true. In the past 10 years, nearly every significant business has been reimagined as a software company, and the market for these companies has, as a result, increased dramatically.

The implication: if there are now 500 important companies per year instead of 15, a generalist $500M fund can't cover the surface area. You need specialists, larger checks, and more capital. So a16z invented something new.

**The multi-fund / sector-fund architecture**

Instead of one fund covering everything, a16z has ~25 distinct funds, each with its own mandate, partners, and LP base. The current active stack looks roughly like this:

- **Core venture funds** (early stage, generalist): Fund I through Fund VIII or so
- **Growth funds** (late-stage, $15B+ across four funds, leading Series C-pre-IPO)
- **American Dynamism** ($600M, defense + aerospace + manufacturing — *this one matters for you*)
- **Bio + Health** (life sciences specialist)
- **Crypto** (multiple vintages, $7.6B+ deployed)
- **Games** ($600M, gaming/entertainment)
- **Apps** ($1B, consumer software)
- **Infrastructure** ($1.25B, AI infra + dev tools)
- **Cultural Leadership Fund** (smaller, brand-building vehicle)
- **Speedrun** (accelerator program, $1M checks across 60 startups per cohort)

Each fund has its own:
- LP base (different LPs care about different sectors)
- Investment partners with domain expertise
- Check size and stage (seed → growth)
- Return expectations and fund cycle

We have just raised $7.2B for American Dynamism ($600M), Apps ($1B), Games ($600M), Infrastructure ($1.25B), and Growth ($3.75B). This marks an important milestone for us.

**Why this structure is genius (and controversial)**

Five reasons it works:

1. **LP optionality.** A pension fund that wants AI exposure but not crypto can buy into the Infrastructure fund without buying the Crypto fund. You're selling tailored exposure, not a generalist box. This dramatically expands your addressable LP universe.

2. **Capital deployment without crushing returns.** A single $20B fund needs $40B+ in exits to be a 2x. A $20B firm split across 8 funds of $2.5B each can each individually be a 3–4x. Smaller funds = better fund-level math, even at large total AUM.

3. **Specialist partners with carry alignment.** A bio investor doesn't want to be diluted by carry on consumer wins they had nothing to do with. Separate funds = separate carry pools = you can recruit world-class specialists.

4. **Reputation arbitrage at scale.** The "a16z brand" wins deals, but each fund competes in its own niche. The brand is shared; the deal competition is segmented. Founders feel like they're getting the specialist *and* the giant.

5. **Different fund cycles.** Funds raised in different vintages spread vintage risk for the firm itself. If 2021 was a bad year to deploy (it was), 2024 funds can recover for the firm even if 2021 funds underperform.

**The platform layer that makes it possible**

a16z also built something nobody else had: a 200+ person operational platform — recruiting, marketing, regulatory affairs, GTM, talent, comms — that serves *all* portfolio companies across all funds. This shared infrastructure is part of why specialist funds can charge 2.5%+ management fees and find LPs willing to pay it. You're not buying a checkbook; you're buying access to a services firm.

**The criticism (and it's valid)**

Many traditionalists argue this isn't really venture capital anymore — it's asset management with a venture wrapper. a16z is doing something different, bigger, less... understated. It doesn't feel like venture capital is supposed to, in part, I think, because I don't think a16z cares if it's doing "venture capital." It just wants to BUILD the future and eat the world.

The concern: when management fees on $90B AUM are roughly $1.8–2.25B/year, the firm is profitable regardless of returns. Critics argue this dulls the incentive to generate carry. Defenders point to AH III's 9–11x TVPI as evidence the math still works.

Time will tell. The 2021–22 vintage funds at a16z are likely underwater right now (true of almost all 2021 funds across the industry). The 2024–26 AI vintage will define whether the multi-fund model holds at scale.

**The follow-on innovation: Speedrun**

Worth flagging this specifically because it shows how the multi-fund model creates new entry points: Launched in 2023 as a gaming-focused program and expanded to all sectors in 2025, Speedrun invests up to $1 million per company across cohorts of 60 startups. This is the fastest entry point into the Andreessen Horowitz portfolio.

Speedrun is a16z's answer to YC. They saw YC owning the seed-stage funnel and built their own. With the multi-fund structure, Speedrun can be its own vehicle with its own LPs, its own carry pool, and its own dedicated team — without diluting any of the other funds.

**What this means for your venture studio path**

Three takeaways relevant to OSA:

1. **You don't need to build a multi-fund firm to be successful, but understand that the modern competitive set has this structure.** When you eventually raise Fund I, you'll be competing for deals against firms that have a defense fund, an AI infra fund, a bio fund, and a growth fund all chasing the same companies. Your edge has to be sharper than theirs — and "deepest aviation domain expertise on Earth" is a defensible answer.

2. **American Dynamism is the fund you'll directly compete with — and potentially LP into, work with, or sell deals to.** Their thesis covers aerospace, defense, manufacturing, robotics, and national interest hard tech. In 2023, the firm launched a $600 million American Dynamism fund "with the broad aim of supporting companies building in the nation's interest". The fund focuses on hard tech sectors which serve national interest, including space, defense, manufacturing, robotics. The fund has backed companies like the defense manufacturing startup Hadrian and the hypersonic long-range rocket startup Castelion. An aviation-tech specialist could legitimately co-invest with American Dynamism on the right deal — they'd want your domain expertise on cap table, you'd want their capital and platform. **This is the relationship to start cultivating now**, even before you're writing checks.

3. **The path from your venture studio to a multi-fund firm exists, but it's 15+ years.** Sequence: studio → Fund I aerospace specialist → Fund II larger → eventual sector expansion (defense fund, eVTOL fund, etc.). Don't try to start with the structure; earn into it. Even a16z started with one $300M fund.

The deeper lesson: **a16z isn't winning because they have a lot of funds. They have a lot of funds because they're winning, and the structure lets them keep winning at scale without the math breaking.** The funds are a consequence of strategic clarity (sector specialization + platform leverage), not the cause of success. Don't copy the structure — copy the underlying strategic insight that domain depth + platform services + brand at scale beats generalist capital.

Good. Here's the actual answer — and I'll separate "famous" from "actually returned the most money," because they're not the same list.

---

**The Midas List top 10 (2025) — the official "best VC" ranking**

Forbes ranks individual investors annually. For the second year running, Alfred Lin of Sequoia Capital tops the list. Lin's long-standing investments in Airbnb and DoorDash continue to bring returns, but it is his 2021 investment in OpenAI at a $14 billion valuation that has provided new momentum. Sequoia dominates the 2025 Midas List with 6 spots, including Alfred Lin at number 1 and Doug Leone at number 7. Hemant Taneja of General Catalyst is ranked #8.

The methodology matters: to qualify, investors must have backed companies that either exited through IPO or acquisition for at least $200 million, or doubled in private value to $400 million or more within the past five years. While both realised and unrealised gains are considered, the ranking places greater weight on realised exits.

So Midas is a 5-year rolling window, weighted toward cash returns. It rewards recency, which is why OpenAI investors dominate 2024–25.

---

**The actual all-time greats (different list)**

The Midas snapshot misses the legends whose biggest exits are older than 5 years. The all-time top tier, by realized returns and historical impact:

**Don Valentine** (founder of Sequoia Capital, 1972)
Backed Apple, Atari, Cisco, Oracle, Yahoo. Built the firm that has dominated venture for 50+ years. Probably the single most important VC who ever lived. Died in 2019.

**Mike Moritz** (Sequoia)
Led seed checks into Google, Yahoo, PayPal, YouTube, LinkedIn, Stripe, Zappos. The Google check alone — Sequoia put in $12.5M, returned ~$4.3B at IPO, ~344x. One of the cleanest career track records in VC history.

**John Doerr** (Kleiner Perkins)
Google, Amazon, Netscape, Compaq, Sun, Twitter. His firm peaked in the 90s/2000s and has since been less dominant, but his individual returns are legendary.

**Doug Leone** (Sequoia)
Co-led Sequoia with Moritz for 25 years. ServiceNow, LinkedIn, Snowflake, Nubank. Doug Leone and Roelof Botha of Sequoia, Navin Chaddha of Mayfield, and Mary Meeker of Bond Capital have all maintained spots on the list for more than a decade.

**Bill Gurley** (Benchmark, recently retired)
Uber, OpenTable, Zillow, Stitch Fix, GrubHub, Nextdoor. Wrote some of the most influential public thinking in venture history through his blog "Above the Crowd." Cultural icon as much as financial one.

**Peter Thiel** (Founders Fund)
Facebook seed (~$500K → $1B+), Palantir co-founder, SpaceX, Stripe, Anduril. Probably the highest IRR of any individual investor ever on his Facebook check alone.

**Marc Andreessen + Ben Horowitz** (a16z)
Already covered. Built the modern VC platform model. Have personally backed Coinbase, Airbnb, GitHub, Databricks, OpenAI, Stripe, Roblox, etc.

**Vinod Khosla** (Khosla Ventures, ex-Kleiner)
Square, DoorDash, Affirm, Instacart, OpenAI, Commonwealth Fusion. Aggressive deep-tech bets. Among the highest absolute return totals of any active VC.

**Roelof Botha** (Sequoia, current managing partner)
YouTube (joined the board pre-Google acquisition), Square, Instagram, MongoDB, 23andMe, Eventbrite. Took over leading Sequoia from Moritz/Leone.

**Reid Hoffman** (Greylock)
LinkedIn founder, then VC. Airbnb, Facebook (angel), OpenAI (board), Aurora. Five members of the "PayPal Mafia," including Reid Hoffman and Peter Thiel, are featured on the list.

---

**The solo angels who became fund-tier successful (relevant to your path)**

This is the list you should actually study, because it's the model your OSA-to-VC path resembles most:

**Elad Gil** — solo investor, no fund mechanics. Gil has made successful investments in defence tech company Anduril, work collaboration tool Glean, and AI search engine Perplexity. His approach, free of investment committees or large partnerships, shows how individual investors can still make a mark. Probably the best modern proof that the venture-studio-of-one model works at the highest level.

**Lachy Groom** — ex-Stripe operator, now solo investor. Backed Notion, Figma, Ramp at very early stages. Eventually formalized into a small fund.

**Naval Ravikant** — AngelList founder. Personal angel portfolio includes Twitter, Uber, Yammer, Notion, Postmates, OpenDoor. Famously didn't run a fund for years; just deployed personal capital with surgical precision.

**Ron Conway** — SV Angel. Decades of tiny early checks across Google, Facebook, Twitter, Airbnb, Pinterest, Square, Stripe. The original "ubiquitous angel" model.

**Pejman Nozad** — Pejman Nozad #1 Seed on the 2025 Midas. Persian rug salesman turned investor, backed Dropbox, Lending Club, DoorDash early. The clearest "outsider becomes legendary VC" story in the industry.

**Aydin Senkut** (Felicis Ventures) — solo angel turned fund. Shopify, Fitbit, Adyen, Credit Karma. Aydin Senkut among honorees on the 2025 list.

---

**The aerospace/deep tech specific list (your domain)**

This is the list to study most carefully because these are your future peers, competitors, co-investors, and potentially LPs:

**Steve Jurvetson** (Future Ventures, ex-DFJ) — SpaceX, Tesla, Planet Labs, Hotmail, Skype. The single most successful aerospace/deep tech investor alive. Board member at SpaceX.

**Katherine Boyle** (a16z, leads American Dynamism) — Anduril, Hadrian, Castelion, Apex Space. Probably the most influential current voice in aerospace/defense venture. Started at General Catalyst, moved to a16z to launch the American Dynamism fund.

**Trae Stephens** (Founders Fund + Anduril co-founder) — bridges operator and investor in the defense tech world. The model of "build a defense company, become its investor side."

**Shaun Maguire** (Sequoia) — SpaceX is now the world's most valuable startup at $350 billion, helping early investors such as Shaun Maguire (80) at Sequoia and David Sacks (85) of Craft Ventures jump into the rankings for the first time. Math PhD, ex-DARPA. Heavy in space/defense/hard tech.

**Delian Asparouhov** (Founders Fund) — Varda Space co-founder + investor. Aerospace specialist at FF.

**Bilal Zuberi** (Lux Capital) — deep tech, robotics, aerospace.

**Josh Wolfe** (Lux Capital co-founder) — Anduril, Hadrian, defense tech generally. Public thinker on hard tech investing.

---

**What "most successful" actually means — and which definition matters for you**

Three definitions, three different lists:

1. **Most realized cash returns to LPs** — Don Valentine, Mike Moritz, Doug Leone, Peter Thiel. The patriarchs.

2. **Highest IRR / multiple on individual deals** — Peter Thiel (Facebook), Mike Moritz (Google), Steve Jurvetson (SpaceX), Reid Hoffman (LinkedIn), Naval Ravikant (Twitter, Uber).

3. **Most influential / structurally important to venture** — Andreessen + Horowitz (platform model), Paul Graham + Sam Altman (YC accelerator model), Bill Gurley (public intellectual), Naval Ravikant (AngelList infrastructure).

For your path, the people to actually study and emulate are different from the headline names. **Study Elad Gil, Naval Ravikant, Lachy Groom, Pejman Nozad, and Katherine Boyle.** Not because they have the biggest absolute numbers, but because their *paths* most resemble the one you can actually run:

- Domain expertise (operator background or specialist focus)
- Concentrated portfolios
- Public thinking that drives inbound dealflow
- Either solo/small structures or sector-specific funds
- Built credibility before scale

The rest of the Midas List won most of their wealth in a venture environment that doesn't exist anymore (consumer internet 1995–2015, mobile 2008–2018). Your environment is AI-native vertical platforms + American Dynamism + the rebuild of physical industries through software. The Boyle / Stephens / Jurvetson / Asparouhov path is much more relevant to your aerospace thesis than the Moritz / Doerr / Valentine path.

**The synthesis worth internalizing**

The most successful VCs aren't the smartest pickers — they're the people who were *in the room* when the generational outcomes happened. Moritz wasn't a better picker than 1000 other VCs in 1999; he was the one Larry and Sergey called. Thiel didn't beat the world to Facebook because of better diligence; he was the one Zuckerberg already knew through PayPal Mafia networks.

For you, "being in the room" runs through OSA being recognized as the aviation-tech platform. Every aerospace founder who eventually builds the next Anduril or Joby will need to talk to the operator who actually understands aviation economics. That's the door OSA opens. The angel checks are downstream of that — they're how you convert "they call you" into "you own a piece of what they build."

Yes — the population is huge. But here's the part that matters: **the population is huge and the *competition that matters* is small.** Those are different statements. Let me break it down.

---

**The raw numbers**

The estimates vary wildly because nobody tracks this cleanly, but here's the range:

- The Small Business Administration estimates over 250,000 active angel investors in the US, providing funding for about 30,000 companies per year
- An estimated 66,000 active angel investors operated in the US, according to historical ACA data, with modest growth projected into 2025
- According to the Center of Venture Research of the University of New Hampshire, the total number of angel investors in the US is 334,680 as of 2020

The huge spread (66K to 335K) tells you something: most "angels" do 1–2 deals and disappear. The 66K number from ACA is closer to people who actually do this regularly.

Capital flowing in: In 2025, US angel investors backed 70,000 funding deals totaling $28 billion. Big number. Sounds saturated.

**But here's the reframe**

At the macro level it looks crowded. At the level you'd actually compete, it isn't. Here's why.

---

**The 99/1 distribution of angels**

Of those 66K–335K "angels":

- ~70% have done 1–3 deals total. They're rich friends-and-family checks. Not real competition for anything.
- ~20% have done 4–15 deals over 5+ years. Casual. Show up at AngelList, write small checks. Not picking sectors deeply.
- ~9% are "professional" angels with 20+ deals and a thesis. Maybe 3,000–6,000 people in the US.
- **~1% are top-tier angels with deep dealflow access, real domain expertise, and the ability to actually help portfolio companies.** Maybe 300–700 people in the entire country.

The top 1% gets >50% of the meaningful dealflow, the meaningful follow-on rights, and the meaningful exits. **The other 99% are statistical noise — they fill out cap tables but don't drive outcomes.**

The data confirms this: the top-ranked angel investors see a full two-thirds of their investments reach some kind of an exit. That's mind-boggling. Bob Pasker tops the list with a reported 75% of his angel investments reaching an exit. An important point to note here is that angels with the highest exit rates tend to invest in later-stage rounds, such as Series A and Series B, rather than in early, pre-seed rounds.

The top angels aren't winning because they pick better — they're winning because they have access to follow-on rounds in already-validated companies. That access comes from reputation and platform, not picking skill.

---

**Sector competition is wildly uneven**

The flood of angels concentrates in maybe four areas: consumer software, B2B SaaS, AI tooling, and crypto. Those are saturated red oceans where 50 angels chase the same Y Combinator deal.

Aviation/aerospace? Different planet entirely. Here's what the competitive landscape actually looks like in your domain:

**Active aerospace/defense angels (US, with real check histories):**

- Steve Jurvetson (SpaceX, Planet) — institutional now
- Trae Stephens (Founders Fund, Anduril co-founder) — institutional
- Katherine Boyle (a16z American Dynamism) — institutional
- Delian Asparouhov (Founders Fund, Varda) — institutional
- Shaun Maguire (Sequoia) — institutional
- Josh Wolfe (Lux Capital) — institutional

Notice the pattern? **All institutional. All at major funds.** There are very few *individual* aerospace angels operating at scale. The space is so capital-intensive and technically demanding that hobbyist angels avoid it. If you can credibly underwrite an aviation startup's certification path, MRO economics, operator math, or pilot supply dynamics, you're competing against maybe 50–100 people in the entire US — most of whom are inside institutional funds with their own dealflow constraints.

For a founder building an aviation-tech company, the realistic angel options are:

1. Raise from generalist angels who don't understand the space (cheap money, no help)
2. Raise from one of the institutional aerospace funds (expensive money, gatekept)
3. Raise from a small handful of operator-angels with real domain expertise (expensive money, deep help)

**Bucket 3 has maybe 20 people in it nationwide. There is no Bucket 3 founder-led-angel for aviation specifically.** Not yet. That's the lane.

---

**What the population numbers actually tell you about your strategy**

Three implications for your path:

**1. Generic angeling is a saturated red ocean. Domain-specialist angeling is empty water.** The 70,000 deals/year happen mostly in SaaS, AI tooling, consumer apps, and DTC. Aerospace dealflow in the US is probably 200–400 deals/year total. The angel-to-deal ratio in your sector is 5–20x more favorable than in SaaS.

**2. The bar to be in the "1% that matters" is not actually that high.** It's: 25+ thoughtful checks, public thinking that demonstrates a thesis, the ability to help portfolio companies with something real, and reputation for fairness with founders. A few thousand people clear this bar nationally. Maybe 30–50 clear it in aerospace specifically. **You can be one of those 30–50 in 5–7 years if OSA gives you the platform.**

**3. The competition that matters is at the LP level, not the deal level.** The real competition isn't other angels chasing your deal — it's: which fund gets to lead the priced round, and do you have the leverage to be in it? When you write the early check at $5M post and the company raises a $30M Series A from a16z American Dynamism a year later, the question is whether they invite you to keep your pro-rata. Whether they do depends on whether Katherine Boyle thinks you're useful to have on the cap table or not. **That's the competition you're actually playing.**

---

**The honest bottom line**

Yes, there are a lot of angels. Most don't matter. The number that actually competes for *good* deals with *real* check sizes in *specific* sectors is much smaller — probably 3,000–6,000 nationwide across all sectors, and 50–100 in aerospace specifically.

Your edge is not "I'll pick better than other angels." Your edge is that **almost no one else can credibly underwrite an aviation-tech investment from inside the cockpit while running a brokerage that talks to the actual customers every day**. That's not a competitive position you have to fight for — it's one you walk into by simply continuing to do what you're already doing at OSA.

The competition feels intense in the abstract because the population is huge. In your specific lane, the competition is sparse and structurally weaker than you'd think. The harder question isn't "can I out-compete other angels" — it's "can I build OSA into a platform meaningful enough that the institutional aerospace investors *want* me on their cap tables." That's a different game, and it's the one worth playing.
