Good — I have the picture. Here's the honest sector breakdown of where VC money goes today, where the historical winners came from, and why the answer matters more than it appears.

---

**The 2025 reality: AI eats everything**

The dollar concentration is unprecedented. In 2025, venture capital investments in AI firms globally made up over half (61%, USD 258.7 billion) of all VC investment (USD 427.1 billion), doubling its 2022 share (30%). And it's getting more concentrated: AI now captures 53% of global VC deal value, up from 32% in Q3 2024. US concentrates funding: Northern America commands nearly 70% of global VC investment in 2025, surging from 57% in 2024 and 48% in 2023.

The breakdown within AI: AI firms focused on IT infrastructure and hosting have attracted the largest volumes of VC, reaching USD 109.3 billion in 2025 alone. Cumulative investment in this sector totalled USD 256.1 billion between 2012 and 2025.

**This is the most concentrated capital allocation in venture history.** AI infrastructure + AI applications + AI-adjacent compute is roughly 50–60% of all VC deployed. Everything else fights over the remaining 40%.

---

**The full sector ranking (2025 deployment, US, approximate)**

In rough order of capital deployed:

1. **AI / ML** — ~$214B (foundation models, infrastructure, applications, AI-native vertical SaaS) — **53–63% of total**
2. **Defense / Aerospace / "American Dynamism"** — ~$25–30B (Anduril, SpaceX, Helsing, Castelion, Apex, Hadrian) — fastest-growing non-AI category, geopolitically tailwind-driven
3. **Healthcare / Biotech** — ~$20–25B (drug discovery, healthtech, AI-bio crossover)
4. **Fintech** — ~$15–20B (down significantly from 2021 peak, but still meaningful)
5. **Climate / Energy** — ~$10–15B (alternative energy, fusion, grid tech)
6. **Crypto / Web3** — ~$8–12B (recovering from 2022 collapse, mostly infrastructure now)
7. **Consumer / Commerce** — ~$6–10B (largely out of favor; small share)
8. **Enterprise SaaS (non-AI)** — ~$5–10B (mostly out of favor unless AI-wrapped)
9. **Frontier (quantum, robotics, biotech moonshots)** — ~$5–8B
10. **Mobility / EVs** — ~$3–5B (cooled significantly from 2021 hype)

Beyond AI, defense technology and space technology garnered significant attention during the quarter, largely due to persistent geopolitical tensions. Health technology, quantum computing, and alternative energy also maintained strong investor interest throughout Q3'25.

---

**Where YC and a16z actually focus**

These are different from each other and worth separating.

**Y Combinator (volume + breadth):**
YC funds 700+ companies/year across nearly every sector, but the current cohort weighting is:
- **AI/ML** — ~70%+ of W25/S25 batches (this is publicly verifiable from their batch listings)
- **B2B SaaS** — heavy, often AI-wrapped
- **Fintech, healthtech, devtools, vertical SaaS** — meaningful share
- **Hard tech** — small but growing (defense, robotics, hardware) via the YC W24/S24 expansion into deep tech
- **Consumer** — minimal these days

YC's approach is "spray broadly, let the power law sort it." Their current AI saturation is so high it's nearly a single-sector accelerator.

**a16z (concentrated + structured):**
Sector-fund AUM allocation gives the cleanest read on their thesis weighting:
- **AI Infrastructure** ($1.25B fund) — top priority
- **Crypto** ($7.6B+ across funds) — largest single thematic bet historically
- **Growth** ($3.75B+) — late-stage AI-heavy
- **Apps** ($1B) — consumer + enterprise applications, AI-wrapped
- **American Dynamism** ($600M) — defense, aerospace, manufacturing, hard tech
- **Bio + Health** — sector specialist fund
- **Games** ($600M) — niche but real
- **Speedrun** — accelerator

a16z's bets reveal the institutional thesis: **AI + Crypto + American Dynamism = the three structural waves of the next decade.** Everything else is supplementary.

---

**Where the historical returns actually came from**

This is the data that contradicts the headline narrative. The biggest VC outcomes of the last 20 years were not from "AI" — they were from:

| Category | Examples | Era |
|---|---|---|
| **Consumer internet / marketplace** | Google, Facebook, Airbnb, Uber, DoorDash | 1998–2015 |
| **Cloud / SaaS infrastructure** | AWS-era SaaS, Snowflake, Databricks, Stripe | 2008–2020 |
| **Mobile** | Instagram, WhatsApp, Snap, Uber | 2008–2018 |
| **Crypto** | Coinbase, ConsenSys, Polygon | 2013–2021 |
| **Hard tech (the dark horse)** | SpaceX, Tesla, Anduril, Palantir | 2002–2025 |
| **AI (current wave)** | OpenAI, Anthropic, Scale, xAI | 2020–present |

Two patterns worth noticing:

**Pattern 1: The biggest outcomes came from sectors that *weren't crowded* when the bet was made.** Consumer internet was unsexy in 1998. SaaS was niche in 2008. Crypto was fringe in 2013. SpaceX was ridiculed in 2002. Anduril was contrarian in 2017. **The capital was deployed when the consensus thought the sector was dumb. The returns came when consensus caught up.**

**Pattern 2: The "obvious" sector at any given moment underperforms its hype.** 2021 crypto = mass dilution. 2022 climate = mostly losses. 2023 generic SaaS = compressed multiples. 2025 AI is showing early signs of the same pattern at the application layer — too many companies, too much capital, too little differentiation.

---

**Where return concentration sits TODAY (the contrarian read)**

The honest answer is split:

**Where the dollars are going (consensus):** AI infrastructure, foundation models, AI applications. Roughly 60% of capital, but probably <30% of next-decade winners by dollar return because valuations are already pricing perfection.

**Where the next decade's outcomes will likely come from (contrarian):**

1. **American Dynamism / hard tech** — Anduril is now $30B+, founded 2017. The next 3–5 will be Castelion, Hadrian, Apex, Saronic, plus aviation/aerospace names yet to emerge. **This is structurally undercrowded. There are roughly 40 active institutional investors in this space versus 4,000+ chasing AI deals.**

2. **AI applied to physical industries (vertical AI)** — not foundation models, but AI-native software running specific industries: aerospace ops, MRO, logistics, manufacturing, energy, agriculture. The "AI eats software" thesis is consensus; "AI runs physical industries" is still contrarian. **OSA is exactly this thesis.**

3. **Bio + AI crossover** — drug discovery, protein design, longevity. Slower exits, but the highest-multiple potential outcomes of the next decade probably come from here.

4. **Energy (nuclear, fusion, grid)** — capital-intensive, long timelines, but the macro pull from AI compute demand + geopolitical energy security creates the strongest macro tailwind in any sector.

---

**What this means for your specific path**

You're sitting at the intersection of two of the strongest thesis vectors of the next decade:

- **American Dynamism / aviation-aerospace** — your domain
- **Vertical AI for physical industries** — what OSA actually is

That intersection is *vastly* less competitive than generic AI investing. Consider the math:

In **AI applications**, you'd be one of 4,000+ angels chasing the same 50 hot AI deals per year. Your edge: zero.

In **aviation-tech / vertical AI for aviation**, you'd be one of maybe 20–30 people in the country with credible domain expertise, looking at maybe 100–200 deals per year. Your edge: structural.

**The biggest sectors are not where you should fight.** They're where the most capital and the most competition concentrate. The Wiltbank data we pulled earlier on angel returns showed sector specialists outperform generalists by roughly 2x in IRR — and that's before adjusting for the difference in deal access and pricing power.

---

**The synthesis worth internalizing**

There are two completely separate questions:

1. *Where is the most VC capital being deployed?* → AI, by a country mile. Followed by Defense/Aerospace, Healthcare, Fintech.

2. *Where will the most concentrated power-law outcomes come from over the next 10 years?* → Probably some combination of: hard tech (American Dynamism), vertical AI in physical industries, bio+AI, and energy. Almost certainly *not* the spaces where current capital is most concentrated.

The classic VC pattern is that **outcomes come from the sectors that were undercrowded 5–10 years before the exit.** Today's undercrowded sectors are aerospace, defense, energy, vertical AI in physical industries, bio. Today's overcrowded sectors are AI applications, fintech, generic SaaS.

**You happen to be domain-credible in one of the most undercrowded sectors with the strongest macro tailwinds.** That's not a coincidence to optimize around — it's the entire thesis. You don't need to compete in YC's or a16z's main lanes. You need to be the obvious answer when an aviation founder is raising and an institutional aerospace fund is looking for the operator they want on the cap table.

The winning move isn't to chase the biggest sector. It's to own the sector where your edge is non-replicable and the macro is bigger than the current capital chasing it.

Want to break down the American Dynamism thesis in detail — what's actually getting funded, what the gaps are, and where an aviation-tech specialist fits in? That's probably the most useful next conversation.

Got the picture. Here are the actual numbers and the meaningful answer to "what's the relevance threshold."

---

**The population (US, 2025–26)**

**VC firms:**
- By the end of 2023, the U.S. VC ecosystem had 3,417 VC firms, which closed a cumulative 13,608 VC deals worth $170.6 billion. These firms raised $66.9 billion across 474 funds and ended the year with a record $311.6 billion in dry powder and a total of $1.21 trillion under management. Median fund size was $35.4 [million] and the largest fund raised in 2023 was $4 billion
- That's roughly stable into 2025–26, though the number has likely contracted 5–10% as zombie funds wind down. Real number today: **~3,000–3,400 active US VC firms**.

**Individual VC investors (partners, principals, associates):**
- ~15,000–20,000 people who professionally allocate venture capital in the US.

**Angel investors:**
- Wide-spread estimates again: ~66,000 active by ACA standards, up to 250–335K if you count anyone who's ever written a check.
- Real "professional" angels (4+ deals, multiple years): probably **3,000–6,000 nationally**.
- Top-tier angels with meaningful access: **300–700 nationally**.

**Sector-specific (aerospace/defense/American Dynamism):**
- Institutional VCs: ~30–50 firms with active aerospace mandates.
- Solo angels with credible domain expertise and active checkbooks: maybe **20–40 individuals nationwide**.

That's the entire competitive set in your specific lane. Less than a hundred people. Most of whom are inside institutional firms with constrained dealflow.

---

**The capital concentration that makes the population numbers misleading**

Here's the part that matters: PitchBook estimates that just 12 firms collected more than 50% of all venture capital raised in H1 2025. The top 1% of AI companies now absorb more than a third of total funding, creating a winner-take-most dynamic reminiscent of the early internet era.

So out of ~3,400 firms:
- **~12 firms** control 50%+ of LP dollars (a16z, Sequoia, Founders Fund, Lightspeed, Khosla, Greylock, Accel, Benchmark, General Catalyst, Tiger, Coatue, Insight)
- **~50 firms** control ~80% of LP dollars
- **~200 firms** are doing serious institutional business
- **The remaining ~3,000** are emerging managers, sub-$50M funds, and zombies

Same pattern in the angel population. Same pattern at the deal level. **Venture is a pyramid, not a flat field.**

---

**What "relevant" actually means — three different thresholds**

This is the question worth unpacking carefully. "Relevant" depends on which game you're playing.

**Threshold 1: "I'm a credible angel that founders take meetings with"**
- 5+ checks deployed, $25K+ average size
- A coherent thesis you can articulate in 60 seconds
- One demonstrably useful skill (domain expertise, network, recruiting, GTM, technical)
- ~$150K+ deployed total

This is a low bar. Maybe 4,000 people clear it. You can clear it in 2–3 years from your OSA position with $150–250K total deployed.

**Threshold 2: "I'm an angel that founders actively want on their cap table"**
- 15–25 checks across multiple vintages
- One meaningful unlock you provide (introductions, customers, technical credibility, brand)
- Public thinking that demonstrates pattern recognition
- $500K–$1.5M deployed total
- At least one marked-up company (3x+ paper)

Maybe 1,000 people in the US clear this bar. You can clear it in 5–7 years if OSA scales and you publish consistently.

**Threshold 3: "I'm an investor whose participation signals validation to other capital"**
- 25–40 checks
- 1–3 deals you led or co-led at the seed
- 1+ company at 30x+ paper or 1 realized exit
- Recognized public voice in your domain
- $2M+ deployed total
- Other VCs proactively invite you to co-invest

Maybe 200–400 people in the US clear this. **This is the threshold where "I want to start a fund" becomes a credible conversation.** You can clear it in 8–12 years from your OSA position.

**Threshold 4: "I'm a fundable first-time GP"**
- All of the above, plus:
- 1+ breakout outcome (50x+ paper or realized) with verifiable check data
- Differentiated thesis with proof points
- LP relationships from your scout/co-investor work
- $3–10M deployed personally with track record

Maybe 50–100 people raise a credible first-time fund per year in the US. The bar is steep and most people who try it fail.

---

**Minimum capital for "relevance" — the honest number**

This is more nuanced than a single dollar figure. Here's the real answer:

**For domain-specialist angel relevance (your realistic target): $1–2M deployed over 5–7 years, ~$30–50K average checks, in your sector.**

That's roughly your post-OSA-success base case from earlier in this conversation. You'd be in the top 1,000 angels in the country and probably the top 5–10 in aerospace specifically. That's enough to compete for and win allocation in the deals that matter to you.

**For generic angel relevance (red ocean): you can't get there.** Spending $5M trying to be a "generalist AI angel" puts you in competition with 5,000 better-positioned people. Your check is invisible. This is why diversification away from your domain edge is actively bad for the GP track.

**For institutional fund-building credibility: $5–15M deployed personally, with at least one breakout, in your domain.**

Roughly your year-10 case if OSA hits the EBITDA you outlined.

---

**Why dollar amounts aren't the binding constraint**

Here's the deeper truth most people miss: capital is not the bottleneck for being a relevant investor. **Access is.** Reputation is. Differentiated dealflow is.

Three illustrative comparisons:

- **Cyan Banister** angeled into SpaceX, Uber, Postmates with checks under $25K each before joining Founders Fund. Total early deployed: ~$300–500K. Now legendary.

- **Naval Ravikant** built his angel reputation with $25–50K checks across Twitter, Uber, Yammer, Postmates. The capital was small. The access was extraordinary.

- **Pejman Nozad**, who topped the 2025 Midas Seed list, started writing $5–10K checks while running a Persian rug shop in Palo Alto. Founders trusted him before he had capital.

Then on the other side: thousands of accredited investors deploy $500K+/year on AngelList Syndicates and remain completely unknown, because they don't have differentiated access or reputation.

**The capital threshold matters, but it's necessary, not sufficient.** What makes you "relevant" is the combination of: deployed capital + sector expertise + public reputation + dealflow access + something useful you provide founders. Dollars are 1 of 5 inputs.

---

**The honest answer for you specifically**

Three things, in order of importance:

**1. Your "relevance threshold" in aerospace is dramatically lower than in AI.** In AI, you'd need $5M+ deployed and a strong track record before founders return your calls. In aerospace, $500K deployed across 10–15 thoughtful checks, plus your operator credibility and OSA platform, makes you one of the more serious individual investors in the entire space. The empty water of your domain is the entire arbitrage.

**2. The path is sequenced, not threshold-based.** You don't cross a single line and become "relevant." You build credibility in stages: 5 checks → 15 checks → led deal → marked-up company → public voice → emerging manager. Each stage compounds the next. Skipping stages by writing huge checks early just wastes capital — founders don't return your call any faster because you wrote $200K instead of $50K. They return your call because you understand their business and have something to offer.

**3. The minimum capital to start being relevant is much smaller than you'd think — somewhere around $200–400K deployed thoughtfully over 2–3 years.** Most aerospace founders would prefer a $25K check from an active commercial pilot running an aviation platform over a $250K check from a generic accredited investor. Your edge is non-fungible. The capital just opens the door; the operator credibility is what keeps you in the room.

**The synthesis**

The VC industry has ~3,000–3,400 firms and ~15,000–20,000 individual investors. The angel industry has 60K–300K depending on how you count. But the **population that competes for the deals you'd actually want** in aerospace is probably under 100 people. The population that's truly fundable as future GPs is probably under 1,000 nationwide.

The minimum capital to be a "relevant angel" in your sector is realistically **$500K–$1M deployed over 3–5 years**, with the right reputation around it. To be a "fundable emerging GP" is roughly **$3–5M deployed over 7–10 years** with at least one strong outcome.

Both are within reach of your post-OSA-success bootstrap model. Neither requires you to compete in the saturated red oceans where most capital and angels concentrate. The single most strategic move you can make right now isn't adjusting your check size or your portfolio plan — it's continuing to make OSA the platform that makes your future capital uniquely useful to the founders you'd want to back.

## Claude

Hold on. Let me push back here, because the ambition is good but the strategy you just described is exactly the trap that kills emerging investors.

**The honest pushback first**

"Be like a16z" and "ride every meaningful wave" sound like the same thing as "be the best." They're not. They're a strategy of *no* concentration, which is a strategy of no edge.

Let me show you why through what a16z actually did, not the brand they project today.

---

**a16z didn't start as a16z**

When Marc and Ben launched in 2009, they had:

- **One $300M generalist tech fund**
- **Two GPs** (themselves)
- **A single thesis:** "software is eating the world" — meaning consumer internet, mobile, SaaS, enterprise software
- **One unfair advantage:** Marc had built Netscape and was one of the few people on Earth who had founded a company that defined an internet wave. Ben had built and sold Opsware to HP for $1.6B. They were operators with founder credibility no other fund partners had.

They were *not* multi-sector. They were *not* covering aerospace, bio, crypto, or anything else. They were focused, narrow, and specifically positioned on one thesis: **"software founders building the next platform companies should call us because we built one and you didn't."**

It took them **14 years** of compounding from that single fund to become the multi-fund firm they are today. Crypto fund came in 2018 (9 years in). Bio in 2015 (6 years). American Dynamism in 2023 (14 years). **Each new fund was launched only after the previous fund proved the platform model worked in a new sector.**

The lesson: **a16z became multi-sector because they earned the right by dominating their first sector.** They didn't start by trying to ride every wave. They started by owning one wave so completely that other waves came to them.

---

**The math problem with "ride every wave"**

Let's run your numbers against this strategy. If you deploy $1.5M/year across 4 sectors equally (AI, SaaS, internet/consumer, aerospace), you'd be putting **$375K/year per sector**. At $30K average checks, that's **12 deals per year, 3 per sector**.

Three deals per year per sector accomplishes nothing. Here's why:

- **In AI**: 3 deals/year is invisible. You're competing against a16z, Sequoia, Khosla, Founders Fund, Lightspeed, plus 100 emerging AI-specialist funds, plus 5,000+ generalist angels. Your $30K check at a hot AI seed round is a rounding error on a $5M round led by a $1B fund. The founder doesn't remember your name.

- **In SaaS**: same dynamic. Bessemer, Iconiq, OpenView, Battery have decades of vertical SaaS expertise. You can't out-pattern-match them with 3 deals/year.

- **In consumer/internet**: dead category for venture in 2026. Capital flows are at multi-year lows. Wrong wave.

- **In aerospace**: 3 deals/year of $30K is ALSO invisible, but for a different reason — the deals are too small, you don't have ownership, and you've spread your bandwidth so thin you can't help any of the companies meaningfully.

**You'd have spent $1.5M/year and ended up in the median-angel bucket in every category. Zero edge in any of them.**

---

**The deeper problem: the waves you're naming are at different points in their cycles**

Let me lay out where each "wave" actually is right now:

**Internet/consumer:** 1995–2015 cycle. Mostly over for venture-scale outcomes. The next consumer wave will probably be AI-native consumer (which is AI, not "internet"). Backing "internet" as a category in 2026 is like backing "railroads" in 1955 — the platform's mature, the returns are gone.

**SaaS:** 2008–2020 cycle. Mostly compressed. Vertical SaaS still works in narrow niches, but multiples have collapsed and AI is eating the application layer. **AI-native SaaS is the live wave; non-AI SaaS is mostly out of favor.** Worth a small allocation if AI-wrapped, otherwise skip.

**AI:** 2020–? cycle. Definitely the live wave. But: PitchBook estimates that just 12 firms collected more than 50% of all venture capital raised in H1 [2025], and the top 1% of AI companies now absorb more than a third of total funding. **Foundation models are out of reach (capital intensity, $5B+ rounds). AI applications are saturated. The interesting AI bet is AI-native vertical companies in physical industries — which is OSA's thesis.**

**Aerospace/American Dynamism:** 2017–present, accelerating. Still early. Anduril founded 2017, now $30B. The next 5–10 years probably produce 3–5 more breakouts at this scale. **Structurally undercrowded** — maybe 30–50 institutional investors versus thousands chasing AI.

The waves aren't equal. They're at totally different points in their cycles. **A relevant strategy doesn't allocate to all waves equally — it overweights the right waves and underweights or skips the wrong ones.**

---

**The strategy that actually rides multiple waves credibly**

Here's the move that gets you what you actually want — relevance across multiple meaningful sectors — without the dilution problem.

**The "concentric circle" approach:**

**Inner circle (60–70% of capital): Vertical AI in aviation/aerospace and adjacent physical industries**

This is your unfair-advantage zone. AI-native software running aviation operations, MRO, defense aerospace, eVTOL fleet management, autonomous flight, sustainable aviation fuel infrastructure, satellite operations, aerospace manufacturing. **It's not "aerospace" — it's "AI applied to aerospace."** That sentence is the entire thesis, and it sits at the intersection of the two strongest waves of the next decade.

This is where your operator credibility, OSA platform, and pilot network give you the deepest competitive moat. You can lead rounds, take board seats, and provide real value. **You can be top-3 nationally in this lane within 5–7 years.**

**Middle circle (20–25% of capital): AI-native software for adjacent physical industries**

Not aerospace, but industries with similar structure: logistics, energy, manufacturing, defense, maritime, autonomous vehicles, robotics. Why these? Because the pattern recognition transfers. The economic model of "AI-native vertical platform displaces legacy middlemen in capital-intensive physical industry" is the same whether it's aviation, trucking, or shipping.

This is where you participate as a thoughtful generalist — you co-invest with specialists in each domain rather than leading. You're not the smartest person on logistics, but you've seen the playbook in aviation and you can pattern-match the economics. **You can be a credible co-investor in the top 5–10% of these deals.**

**Outer circle (10–15% of capital): Pure-play AI infrastructure and frontier**

This is your "be in the room" allocation. Small checks ($25–50K) into the highest-quality AI infrastructure deals you can access — typically through scout programs, syndicates led by domain specialists, or co-invest rights from your inner-circle relationships. You're not picking. You're following the best AI investors and getting exposure.

**You will not be relevant in pure AI investing.** Nobody in your trajectory will be. Foundation models are inaccessible. AI infrastructure is institutionally captured. The best you can do is small exposure through the right syndicates, treating it as portfolio diversification rather than strategic positioning.

---

**Why this beats the "ride every wave" approach**

The concentric circle structure gets you relevance in three sectors *because* it doesn't try to compete in all three on equal footing.

- **Aerospace + AI-native vertical:** You compete and win because of structural advantage.
- **Adjacent physical industries:** You participate and learn because the pattern transfers.
- **Pure AI:** You get exposure and signal without trying to compete.

This is what every successful multi-sector investor actually built. **Marc Andreessen didn't start covering crypto until he had earned the right.** Peter Thiel's early angel period was concentrated in PayPal Mafia connections — Facebook, LinkedIn, Yelp — not "every wave." Vinod Khosla started narrow at Sun Microsystems network adjacency before expanding to clean tech and then deep tech.

**Multi-sector relevance is a consequence of dominating one sector first, not a starting strategy.**

---

**The harder truth about "being like a16z"**

a16z's structural advantages aren't replicable by one person, ever. Three reasons:

**1. Platform leverage requires institutional scale.** a16z has 200+ operational staff (recruiting, marketing, regulatory, comms, talent). That's a $50–100M/year operating expense. They support portfolio companies in ways no individual ever can. You cannot replicate "the platform" alone.

**2. Brand was built over 30 years of founder reputation.** Marc and Ben's credibility from Netscape and Opsware predated the firm. The "a16z brand" sells partly because Marc Andreessen *invented the modern web browser*. That credibility seeds the firm's deal access. Your equivalent path is "Phelipe built the dominant aviation-tech platform of the 2020s" — that's the OSA outcome, and it's exactly the kind of credential that supports a multi-sector firm later. **But the credential has to come from operating, not from investing.**

**3. AUM scale enables multi-fund mechanics.** You cannot run sector-specific funds with separate LP bases until you have enough total AUM that LPs can choose between them. That's a $500M+ AUM problem, which is fund III or IV territory at the earliest. **You earn into it; you don't start there.**

---

**The realistic version of "being like a16z" for your trajectory**

Here's what the 15-year arc could actually look like:

**Years 1–5 (current): OSA dominance phase.**
Don't deploy any angel capital yet beyond opportunistic operator-friend checks. Build OSA into the recognized aviation-tech platform. Build Loud Hawk into the recognized aviation-tech publication. Establish "Phelipe = aviation operator with deep AI/tech understanding."

**Years 5–10: Concentrated angel + sector authority phase.**
$1–2M/year deployed, 70% inner circle (vertical AI in aerospace), 20% middle circle (AI in adjacent physical industries), 10% pure AI exposure. Build the track record: 25+ checks, 2–4 led rounds, 1–2 marked-up companies, 1 breakout potential. Become the recognized investor voice in aviation-tech. **You are now a top-5 specialist investor in your sector and a credible voice in adjacent sectors.**

**Years 10–13: Emerging manager phase.**
Raise Fund I — $25–50M, sector-specific (American Dynamism for aviation/aerospace, possibly with explicit AI-native thesis). Outside LPs, co-investing with a16z, Founders Fund, Lux on the deals you source. **You are now an institutional investor.**

**Years 13–18: Multi-fund expansion phase.**
Fund II is bigger ($75–150M) and possibly splits: a flagship aerospace fund and a thesis-adjacent fund (AI-native physical industries broadly, or a specific adjacency like defense or energy). You hire your first 1–2 partners — operators from your portfolio, not VCs. **You begin platform leverage.**

**Years 18+: Multi-sector firm.**
Now you've earned what a16z earned. Sector-specific funds, multiple GPs, platform staff, multi-billion AUM. You can credibly cover aerospace, AI-applied-to-physical, defense, and frontier hard tech because you've earned each lane through track record and team-building.

**This is a 20-year arc.** That's what "being like a16z" actually means as a path. They've been at it 17 years and are still consolidating.

---

**The hardest sentence in this conversation**

The desire to "ride every meaningful wave" usually masks something worth examining honestly: **a discomfort with concentration risk.** Concentration feels scary because if your one bet is wrong, you lose. So the instinct is to spread across multiple bets.

But concentration is how generational outcomes are built. Bezos concentrated on Amazon. Elon concentrated on Tesla and SpaceX. Naval concentrated on AngelList. Marc Andreessen concentrated on Netscape, then on a16z. The Founders Fund partners concentrated on the PayPal Mafia network. Every "be like a16z" outcome started from extreme concentration that earned the right to expand later.

**You already have a concentration that's working: OSA + aviation + AI-native vertical platforms.** The most valuable thing about your position right now is that you're at the intersection of two of the strongest waves of the next decade, with operator credibility almost no one else has. The strategic question isn't "how do I diversify into more waves" — it's "how do I press the advantage I already have so hard that other waves come to me later."

The tactical answer to your question: stay concentrated in aviation/aerospace AI for the next 7–10 years, expand into adjacent physical-industry AI as your reputation compounds, take small exposure to pure AI through syndicates without trying to compete, and earn the right to a multi-sector firm by dominating your first sector first. **In 15 years, you can be running multiple funds covering multiple meaningful sectors. In year 1 of your investing career, trying to do that costs you the only edge you have.**

The wave you want to ride isn't AI broadly. It isn't aerospace broadly. It's "AI-native vertical platforms in physical industries, starting with aviation." That sentence is more specific than a16z's was in 2009, and it's a wave that is structurally bigger than most people realize. Own it. Then ride others later, after you've earned that right.

## Phelipe

What are the other sector that funds are good. Like dtc etc?

---

## Claude

Good — let me lay out the full sector landscape honestly, including which ones are dead, which are quietly working, and which only look attractive. DTC is actually a great example of a sector that almost everyone gets wrong right now.

---

**DTC: dead as a venture category, alive as a business category**

This distinction is critical and most people miss it.

**Why DTC died as a VC sector:**
The 2014–2021 DTC wave (Warby Parker, Casper, Allbirds, Dollar Shave Club, Glossier, Harry's, Away, Outdoor Voices, Hims) was built on a temporary arbitrage: Facebook/Instagram CAC was cheap because the platforms were under-monetized. That arbitrage closed around 2018. Then Apple's iOS 14 privacy changes in 2021 destroyed targeting precision. Then post-COVID supply chain costs blew up unit economics.

The result: Casper IPO'd at $14, trades sub-$5. Allbirds IPO'd at $15, hit $0.80 before going private. Outdoor Voices imploded. Away laid off most of its staff. Even Warby Parker — the supposed DTC poster child — is barely break-even on adjusted EBITDA after 15 years. The exits never came.

**The math venture cares about:** Casper raised $340M+ in venture funding, IPO'd at $1.1B, and is now valued at $300M private. That's a ~70% loss for late-stage investors. The category is now actively avoided by most VCs.

**Why DTC is alive as a business category:**
Profitable DTC brands exist and thrive — they just don't take venture money. Examples: True Classic (~$300M revenue, profitable, bootstrapped), Vuori, Alo Yoga, Ridge Wallet, Fashion Nova, hundreds of niche brands doing $10–100M revenue with 20%+ margins. **They run on cash flow and either stay private or sell to PE/strategics for 5–10x EBITDA, not 10x revenue.**

The lesson: DTC is now a "small business" category, not a venture category. **You should not put angel money into DTC startups expecting venture-scale outcomes.** If you want DTC exposure, buy public consumer ETFs or invest in private equity rollups.

---

**The actual sector landscape, ranked by venture relevance in 2026**

Let me give you the full honest map, organized by which waves are live, dying, dead, or contrarian-interesting.

---

**LIVE WAVES (where capital deploys and outcomes are forming)**

**1. AI infrastructure + foundation models**
Status: massively overheated but real.
Examples: OpenAI, Anthropic, xAI, Mistral, Scale AI, Groq, CoreWeave, Together AI.
Capital intensity: prohibitive ($1B+ rounds standard). Inaccessible at angel scale.
Your play: small syndicate exposure only. Don't try to compete.

**2. Vertical AI / AI-native applications**
Status: live, less crowded than infrastructure.
Examples: Harvey (legal AI), Hippocratic (healthcare), Sierra (customer service), Glean (enterprise search), Cursor (coding), plus thousands of vertical-specific plays.
Capital intensity: reasonable ($5–30M Series A typical).
Your play: this is where your inner-circle thesis lives — AI-native applied to aerospace, defense, logistics, energy, manufacturing.

**3. American Dynamism (defense, aerospace, manufacturing, hard tech)**
Status: live and accelerating, geopolitically tailwind-driven.
Examples: Anduril, Hadrian, Castelion, Apex Space, Saronic, Helsing, Shield AI, Vannevar Labs, Palmer Luckey's broader network.
Capital intensity: high but venture-fundable.
Your play: this is your sector. Aviation-tech sits inside this category.

**4. Defense tech specifically (subset of above, worth separating)**
Status: white-hot. Anduril's $2.5B round in 2025. Multiple unicorns forming.
Examples: Anduril, Shield AI, Palantir alumni networks, Vannevar, Mach Industries.
Macro driver: $850B+ US defense budget reorienting toward software-defined systems.
Your play: extremely relevant, adjacent to aviation. Maybe 10–15% allocation.

**5. Energy / nuclear / fusion**
Status: live and growing rapidly. Massive macro tailwind from AI compute power demand.
Examples: Commonwealth Fusion Systems, Helion, Oklo, X-Energy, Type One Energy, plus grid infrastructure plays like Camus.
Capital intensity: very high. Long timelines (10–15 year exits).
Your play: small exposure through specialist funds. Real but slow.

**6. Bio + AI crossover**
Status: live, recovering from 2022–24 biotech downturn.
Examples: Recursion, Insilico, Isomorphic Labs, Generate Biomedicines, Xaira.
Capital intensity: high. Specialized expertise required.
Your play: probably skip unless you have a specific thesis. Generalist exposure underperforms.

**7. Robotics / autonomy (physical AI)**
Status: emerging, fast-growing.
Examples: Figure AI, 1X, Skild, Physical Intelligence, plus autonomous vehicle survivors.
Capital intensity: high. Hardware-heavy.
Your play: relevant — aerospace overlaps significantly with autonomy. Worth tracking carefully.

---

**DYING WAVES (capital still flows but outcomes are compressing)**

**8. Generic enterprise SaaS (non-AI)**
Status: largely cooked. Multiples compressed. For early-stage founders outside of AI, the fundraising environment remains competitive. Strong fundamentals – recurring revenue, retention, and capital efficiency – are essential regardless of sector.
Why dying: AI is eating SaaS margins. Software companies are being asked "are you AI-native?" and pure-SaaS companies struggle to answer well.
Your play: skip unless AI-wrapped.

**9. Fintech (broadly)**
Status: post-bubble correction. Some pockets alive (B2B payments, infrastructure, AI-enabled underwriting), most consumer fintech dead.
Examples that worked: Stripe, Plaid, Ramp, Mercury, Brex.
Examples that didn't: dozens of consumer neobanks, BNPL me-toos, crypto-fintech crossovers.
Your play: limited and selective. AI-enabled vertical fintech has some legs (your OSA Pay thesis is actually one of these).

**10. Crypto / Web3**
Status: recovered from 2022 collapse but capital deployment is selective. Mostly infrastructure plays.
Examples: Coinbase ecosystem, ETH staking, Bitcoin treasury companies, stablecoin infrastructure.
Capital intensity: variable.
Your play: skip unless you have specific conviction. Domain knowledge is high and you don't have it.

---

**DEAD WAVES (avoid)**

**11. DTC consumer brands** (covered above)

**12. Generic consumer mobile apps**
The 2008–2018 consumer mobile wave is over. App store distribution is captured. CAC economics are broken. Outliers exist (BeReal failed, Locket failed, Marco Polo struggling) but venture-scale outcomes are extremely rare.

**13. Marketplaces (most flavors)**
Most horizontal marketplaces are saturated. Vertical marketplaces in unexplored niches still work but are rare. Your OSA Connect is technically a marketplace but it's really an AI-native vertical platform — different category.

**14. Adtech**
Permanently displaced by Meta/Google duopoly + privacy regulations. Capital flees the category.

**15. Education tech (most variants)**
2020–2022 wave overinvested. Most consumer EdTech is dead. AI-native learning tools (like Khan Academy's Khanmigo, MagicSchool) are interesting but it's a brutal sector with weak unit economics.

**16. Food delivery, micro-mobility, gig economy**
The 2017–2020 wave produced DoorDash and not much else. Capital has fled.

**17. Metaverse / VR / AR (consumer)**
The 2021 wave collapsed. Apple Vision Pro and Meta Quest haven't catalyzed it. Some industrial AR has legs. Consumer side is dead.

---

**CONTRARIAN INTERESTING (less crowded, real macro)**

**18. Climate tech / industrial decarbonization**
Status: down from 2022 peak but maturing into real industries. Less hype, more execution.
Examples: Form Energy, Sublime Systems, Boston Metal, plus dozens of grid/storage plays.
Why contrarian: capital fled after 2022 hype reset. Survivors are now more attractive.
Your play: small allocation if you find specific angles. Adjacent to your aerospace thesis (sustainable aviation fuel, electric aviation).

**19. Vertical AI for legal, healthcare, finance, real estate, construction**
Status: emerging. Less competition than horizontal AI.
Examples: Harvey (legal), Hippocratic (health), AlphaSense (research), Ironclad.
Why contrarian: domain expertise creates moats horizontal AI doesn't have.
Your play: maybe small allocation. The pattern recognition transfers from your aviation work.

**20. Space (commercial, beyond SpaceX)**
Status: maturing from speculative to real businesses.
Examples: Varda Space, Apex, K2 Space, Stoke Space, Astranis, Albedo, Hadrian.
Why interesting: declining launch costs unlock real commercial use cases.
Your play: directly aerospace-adjacent. Strong fit for your portfolio.

**21. Industrial AI / manufacturing tech**
Status: emerging. Hadrian-style "AI-native factory" companies.
Examples: Hadrian, Flow, Path Robotics, Machina Labs.
Why interesting: massive TAM, almost no early-stage competition.
Your play: very relevant — aerospace overlaps directly with industrial manufacturing.

**22. Healthcare operations / care delivery**
Status: undercrowded relative to opportunity. Operationally complex which scares generalist VCs.
Examples: Devoted Health, Cityblock, Olive (failed), General Catalyst's healthcare AI roll-up strategy.
Your play: probably skip unless you have specific conviction. Domain knowledge is high.

**23. Frontier (quantum, advanced materials, longevity)**
Status: very early. Long timelines.
Examples: PsiQuantum, Atom Computing, Altos Labs, Xaira.
Your play: small exposure through specialist funds only.

---

**The pattern worth noticing**

Look at which categories died and which survived. The common thread:

**Categories that died had no defensibility against either AI or platform consolidation:**
- DTC: no moat against AI-driven personalization at scale by Amazon, Walmart
- Adtech: crushed by Meta/Google duopoly
- Consumer mobile: captured by app store gatekeepers
- Generic SaaS: AI rewriting the application layer

**Categories that thrive have either:**
- Hard physical/regulatory moats (aerospace, defense, energy, industrial)
- Domain depth that AI can't easily replicate without deep operator knowledge (vertical AI for complex industries)
- Capital intensity that creates winner-take-most dynamics (foundation models, infrastructure, energy)
- Geopolitical/macro tailwinds (American Dynamism, defense, energy security)

**The pattern: software-only consumer plays are mostly dead. Software-meets-physical is the live game.** This is why OSA's positioning is structurally smart — you're at the intersection of AI software and physical aviation, in a domain with regulatory complexity that creates a moat.

---

**The honest portfolio implication**

For someone in your trajectory, the relevant sector allocation isn't "ride every wave." It's:

- **Aerospace + American Dynamism: 50–60%** — your edge zone
- **Vertical AI in physical industries (logistics, energy, manufacturing, defense): 20–25%** — pattern transfers from aviation
- **Pure AI exposure through syndicates: 10–15%** — be in the room, don't compete
- **Climate / energy / contrarian frontier: 5–10%** — adjacent macro
- **Everything else (DTC, fintech, crypto, consumer, generic SaaS): 0%** — explicitly skip

That last line is the hardest one to write but the most important. **Saying no to 70% of the venture market is what makes the 30% you say yes to actually deliver.**

The temptation to do "a little bit of consumer" or "a little bit of fintech" comes from FOMO, not strategy. There's no version of writing $25K consumer angel checks that helps your GP path. There IS a version of writing zero consumer checks and concentrating that capital in your aviation/aerospace edge zone that gets you to fundable emerging manager status in 8–10 years.

**The discipline to skip dead and dying waves is what creates the surplus to dominate live ones.**

## Claude

Good question — and the framing matters. There are really two different worlds: **the venture-funded categories** (where VCs deploy serious capital) and **the alternative-funded categories** (where consumer/CPG/lifestyle businesses actually get money). A founder pitching a beverage in 2026 is mostly in the second world, not the first. Let me lay it all out.

---

**The VC category map (what VCs actually have funds for)**

Most large VCs organize internally into "practice areas" or "verticals." Here's the actual taxonomy used by the major firms today:

**Tech / Software**
- AI / ML (foundation models, infrastructure, applications)
- Enterprise SaaS / B2B software
- Developer tools / infrastructure
- Cybersecurity
- Vertical software (industry-specific SaaS)
- Productivity / collaboration

**Fintech**
- Payments / B2B payments
- Banking infrastructure (BaaS, neobanks)
- Insurance tech
- Lending / credit
- Capital markets / wealth tech
- Crypto / blockchain

**Consumer Tech**
- Consumer social / mobile apps
- Marketplaces
- Creator economy
- Gaming
- Consumer subscription / DTC tech (the *technology* layer, not brands)

**Healthcare / Life Sciences**
- Biotech / drug discovery
- Digital health / care delivery
- Medical devices
- Health insurance / payer tech
- Healthcare AI

**Hard Tech / Frontier**
- Aerospace / space
- Defense / national security ("American Dynamism")
- Robotics / autonomy
- Climate / energy / fusion / nuclear
- Quantum / advanced materials
- Industrial / manufacturing tech

**Mobility**
- Autonomous vehicles
- EV infrastructure
- Logistics / supply chain tech

**Real Estate / Proptech**
- Construction tech
- Real estate marketplaces
- Property management software

**Education**
- AI tutoring
- Workforce / upskilling
- (Mostly out of favor)

**Web3 / Crypto** (often its own fund)
- Infrastructure
- DeFi
- Consumer crypto applications

That's roughly the full taxonomy. Notice what's *not* on the list: beverages, food brands, cosmetics, fashion, fitness studios, restaurants, hotels, retail concepts, supplements, personal care brands, household goods. **None of those are venture categories.** They're consumer brand categories, which is a fundamentally different game.

---

**The harder truth: most "consumer" categories are not VC-fundable**

This is the part most first-time founders get wrong. They see Glossier and think "beauty is venture-fundable." They see Liquid Death and think "beverages are venture-fundable." Both are statistical anomalies that distract from the actual base rate.

**The 2014–2021 DTC era created a misleading impression that consumer brands could be venture businesses.** That era is over. The exits didn't materialize. The capital fled. Today, consumer brand investing has largely returned to its historical home: family offices, consumer-focused private equity, and specialized CPG accelerators.

**Why consumer brands struggle as venture investments (the honest economics):**

- **TAM ceilings:** A great beverage brand might do $200M revenue, sell for $500M–$1B. That's a great outcome for the founder. For a venture fund that owns 10% of a $20M-raised company, that's a 5x return — fine, but not the 100x that makes the power-law math work.
- **Capital efficiency mismatch:** Consumer brands need lots of working capital (inventory, distribution, marketing) but produce slow, linear growth. Venture wants the opposite: low capital intensity and exponential growth.
- **No defensibility:** A brand's moat is taste and distribution. AI doesn't displace it, but neither does software compound the moat. There's no "network effect" in beverages.
- **Exit comp is M&A, not IPO:** Consumer brands almost always sell to strategics (PepsiCo, Unilever, P&G, L'Oréal). Strategic acquisition prices are 1–4x revenue or 8–15x EBITDA. Those multiples don't support venture math.

**The beverage exits that actually happened recently:**

- Liquid Death: $1.4B private valuation, hasn't exited yet (2024 round)
- Olipop: ~$850M valuation, hasn't exited (2025)
- Poppi: acquired by PepsiCo for $1.95B (March 2025) — the headline outcome
- Bai Brands: sold to Dr Pepper Snapple for $1.7B in 2017
- Vita Coco: IPO'd, market cap ~$2B
- Bodyarmor: sold to Coca-Cola for $5.6B in 2021

These are great outcomes. But notice: **most are PE-backed or family-office-backed, not VC-backed.** Bodyarmor's biggest individual investor was Kobe Bryant ($6M for ~10%, returned ~$400M — better than most VCs in their entire career, but it was a personal angel bet, not a fund). Liquid Death is backed by a mix of PE, celebrity capital, and consumer-specialist funds — not generalist VCs.

---

**Where a beverage founder actually pitches in 2026**

Different stages, different funders. Here's the realistic capital map:

**Pre-seed / seed ($100K–$2M): friends, family, and angel networks**
- Founder + family money
- Industry angels (former CPG executives — there's a robust ecosystem of these)
- Local / regional accelerators (SKU, Chobani Incubator)
- Some celebrity/athlete angels who specifically invest in CPG

**Seed / Series A ($1–10M): consumer-specialist funds**
- **CAVU Consumer Partners** (backed Vital Proteins, Bulletproof, Hu Kitchen)
- **VMG Partners** (Drunk Elephant, Health-Ade)
- **L Catterton** (very large consumer PE/VC hybrid)
- **Sonoma Brands**
- **Selva Ventures**
- **Beechwood Capital**
- **Powerplant Partners**
- **Stripes** (later stage, consumer-tech focused)
- **Greycroft Consumer**
- **Lerer Hippeau** (consumer-leaning early-stage NYC fund)
- Plus 30–50 smaller consumer-specialist funds

**Growth / scale ($10–50M): consumer-focused growth equity**
- L Catterton (primary player)
- TSG Consumer Partners
- Encore Consumer Capital
- Verlinvest (the InBev family office, very active in beverages)
- Mainsail Partners
- KarpReilly

**Strategic / pre-exit:**
- Coca-Cola Venturing & Emerging Brands
- PepsiCo Greenhouse (their incubation arm)
- Anheuser-Busch InBev's ZX Ventures
- Constellation Brands' Wildcat Capital
- Unilever Ventures
- L'Oréal BOLD
- These are corporate VCs that often act as a stepping stone to acquisition

**Alternative / non-VC paths (often the right answer):**
- **Revenue-based financing** (Pipe, Clearco, Wayflyer for inventory/marketing)
- **Specialty CPG lenders** (Crayhill, Stenn)
- **Crowdfunding** (Republic, Wefunder — actually works for consumer brands)
- **Bootstrapping with Amazon/Shopify infrastructure**
- **Family offices** (vastly more important than VCs in CPG)

---

**The full picture of where capital actually lives by category**

Let me give you the honest answer to "if someone wants to pitch X, who do they go to" across all the major categories:

| If pitching... | Right primary funder |
|---|---|
| AI infrastructure / foundation model | a16z, Sequoia, Founders Fund, Khosla, NEA |
| AI vertical SaaS | Same firms + sector specialists (Bessemer for SaaS, OpenView, Insight) |
| Defense / aerospace | Founders Fund, Lux, a16z American Dynamism, 8VC, Shield Capital |
| Climate / fusion | Breakthrough Energy Ventures, Khosla, Lowercarbon, Energy Impact Partners |
| Biotech | ARCH, Flagship, Third Rock, OrbiMed, RA Capital |
| Fintech | Ribbit, QED, Index, Bain Capital Ventures |
| Crypto | Paradigm, a16z Crypto, Multicoin, Variant |
| Consumer brand (beverage, food, beauty, apparel) | CAVU, VMG, L Catterton, family offices, NOT generalist VCs |
| Restaurants / hospitality | Almost never venture; debt, franchising, PE |
| Media / content | Mostly out of favor; sometimes a16z Cultural Leadership Fund |
| Real estate / construction | Fifth Wall, MetaProp, Camber Creek |
| Healthcare services | General Catalyst Health Assurance, Oak HC/FT, .406 Ventures |
| Gaming | a16z Games, Bitkraft, Makers Fund, Galaxy Interactive |
| Robotics | Lux, Founders Fund, Eclipse, Khosla |
| Education | Reach Capital, Owl Ventures, GSV |
| B2B SaaS (general) | Bessemer, Insight, ICONIQ, Iconiq, Battery |
| Vertical SaaS | Bessemer, OMERS Ventures, vertical-specific funds |
| Marketplaces | Index, Greylock, Benchmark (selective now) |
| Cybersecurity | Lightspeed, Battery, Insight, Ten Eleven |

---

**The takeaway for the "what about a beverage" question**

If a friend came to you with a beverage pitch, the honest counsel would be:

1. **Don't pitch generalist VCs.** They will pass, and your time is better spent on the right capital.
2. **The right capital for a beverage in 2026 is consumer-specialist PE/VC (CAVU, VMG, L Catterton), strategic corporate venture arms (Coca-Cola, PepsiCo, AB InBev), CPG-focused angels, and family offices.**
3. **The exit path is acquisition by a strategic, not IPO.** Build to be acquired. Optimize for distribution (retail relationships) and brand equity, not for "venture-scale unicorn outcome."
4. **Capital structure should match the business model.** Use revenue-based financing for inventory and marketing. Take equity only when needed for category creation or major distribution expansion.
5. **The base rate is sobering:** roughly 95% of consumer brands fail or stay sub-scale. The 5% that win do so through extraordinary brand discipline + retail distribution + product market fit, not through capital. Throwing more money at a struggling consumer brand rarely fixes it.

---

**The deeper sector-strategy lesson for you**

The reason this distinction matters for your own thinking: **knowing what's NOT a venture category is half the strategy.** You can't be a relevant investor in beverages because beverages aren't a venture category. The right player in beverages is L Catterton, not you, and that's fine — there's no aspiration to compete there.

Same logic applies to your sector concentration. You're not "missing out" on consumer brands by skipping them. You'd be deploying capital into a category where venture math doesn't work, where your edge is zero, and where the right investors are structurally different than what you'd ever build.

The categories where venture math DOES work — hard tech, AI, vertical software in physical industries, defense, energy — happen to be the categories where your operator credibility from OSA is most valuable. **The map of "where venture works" and "where your edge lives" overlap heavily, by design, not by accident.**

That overlap is the entire reason OSA → angel program → emerging manager fund is a coherent strategy. If you tried to be relevant in beverages or consumer brands, you'd be fighting on terrain where neither venture economics nor your personal advantage applies. The discipline to recognize that and stay out is what makes the strategy work.

## Phelipe

So let's think. What are the categories. And who invests in who. Because VC and PE are different right?

---

## Claude

Right — and this is the most useful structural question you've asked. Most people conflate VC, PE, and the layers in between, but they're completely different vehicles with different math, different stages, and different deal types. Let me lay out the full capital landscape clearly.

---

**The capital stack: who funds what, when, and how**

Capital for businesses isn't one market. It's a stack of vehicles, each with its own job. The same company often touches 4–6 different types of capital across its life. Here's the full picture:

---

**Stage 1: Pre-revenue / earliest stage**

**Friends, family, and founder capital**
- Check size: $10K–$200K
- Equity given: variable, often informal
- Who: parents, friends, founder savings
- When: idea stage to first product

**Angel investors (individual)**
- Check size: $5K–$250K (typical $25–50K)
- What they want: equity, usually via SAFEs or convertible notes
- Who: wealthy individuals, often former operators
- When: pre-seed to early seed

**Pre-seed funds**
- Check size: $250K–$1.5M
- Examples: Hustle Fund, Pear VC, Forum Ventures, Backstage Capital, AfroTech
- When: idea/early product, before product-market fit

**Accelerators**
- Check size: $125K–$500K (in exchange for 5–10% equity)
- Examples: Y Combinator ($500K for 7%), Techstars, 500 Global, plus sector-specific (Boost VC for hard tech, AlchemistX for B2B)
- When: idea to early traction

---

**Stage 2: Early venture (seed to Series B)**

**Seed funds**
- Check size: $500K–$5M
- Fund size: $50M–$300M typically
- Examples: First Round, Initialized, Bloomberg Beta, K9, Susa Ventures
- What they want: 10–20% ownership at $5–20M post-money

**Multi-stage VCs (early-stage practice)**
- Check size: $2M–$20M
- Fund size: $300M–$3B
- Examples: a16z, Sequoia, Founders Fund, Benchmark, Greylock, Accel, Lightspeed
- These are the firms most people mean when they say "VC"

**Sector-specialist VCs**
- Check size: $1M–$15M
- Examples: Bessemer (cloud), Ribbit (fintech), Lux (deep tech), Paradigm (crypto), Andreessen American Dynamism (defense)
- More disciplined thesis, deeper expertise, often better partners for domain-heavy companies

**Corporate VCs (CVCs)**
- Check size: $500K–$50M
- Examples: GV (Google), M12 (Microsoft), Salesforce Ventures, Intel Capital, Nvidia, Coca-Cola VEB
- Strategic capital — they invest for both returns and strategic insight/access for the parent company

---

**Stage 3: Growth (Series C to pre-IPO)**

**Growth equity firms**
- Check size: $20M–$500M
- Targets: profitable or near-profitable companies, $20M+ revenue, growing 30–80%/year
- Examples: General Atlantic, Summit Partners, TA Associates, Insight Partners, Stripes, JMI, Susquehanna Growth Equity
- Style: less risky than VC, more risky than PE. Often take significant equity but rarely control.

**Crossover funds**
- Check size: $25M–$500M+
- Examples: Tiger Global, Coatue, D1, Whale Rock, T. Rowe Price, Fidelity, Wellington
- These are public-market hedge funds that also invest in late-stage private companies. They drove the 2020–2021 valuation bubble. Less active in 2024–2026 after big losses.

**Sovereign wealth funds**
- Check size: $50M–$1B+
- Examples: Saudi PIF, Abu Dhabi's Mubadala, Singapore's GIC and Temasek, Norway's Norges Bank
- Massive checks, very long time horizons, often anchor mega-rounds

---

**Stage 4: Private equity (PE) — completely different game**

This is where it diverges from VC fundamentally. PE is not a continuation of venture; it's a different asset class entirely.

**Buyout PE (the big leagues)**
- Check size: $100M–$15B+ (whole-company purchases)
- Targets: profitable, mature companies. Usually $20M+ EBITDA. Often takes companies private.
- Examples: Blackstone, KKR, Carlyle, Apollo, TPG, Bain Capital, Vista Equity, Thoma Bravo, Silver Lake
- What they want: control (51%+ usually), board seats, ability to install management
- Returns target: 2.5–4x money in 5–7 years (15–25% IRR)
- Strategy: buy with leverage, improve operations, sell or IPO

**Mid-market PE**
- Check size: $25M–$300M
- Targets: $10–100M EBITDA companies
- Examples: Audax, Genstar, GTCR, Berkshire Partners
- Same playbook as buyout but for smaller companies

**Lower-middle-market PE**
- Check size: $5M–$50M
- Targets: $2–15M EBITDA, often family businesses or founder-owned
- Examples: hundreds of regional firms; this is a fragmented space
- Style: roll-ups, platform-and-add-on strategies

---

**Stage 5: The specialized vehicles in between**

**Sector-specific PE/VC hybrids**
- Examples: L Catterton (consumer), Vista Equity (software-only), Thoma Bravo (software-only), Clayton Dubilier (industrials), Roark Capital (restaurants/franchising)
- Look like PE in structure but invest more selectively in specific sectors

**Family offices**
- Check size: $250K–$100M+, depending on the family
- The wealth manager for billionaire families
- Often invest directly into private companies, not just through funds
- Examples: Walton Enterprises (Walmart heirs), Pritzker family, Cargill-MacMillan, Mars, Wertheimer (Chanel), plus thousands of $50M–$5B family offices most people have never heard of
- Critical for consumer brands — they're patient capital that doesn't need 10x venture returns

**Search funds**
- Check size: $5M–$50M (single acquisition target)
- Niche vehicle: an MBA grad raises capital to find ONE company to buy and run
- Targets: $1–5M EBITDA, "boring" businesses (HVAC, plumbing, B2B services)
- Examples: Pacific Lake Partners, Search Fund Partners
- This is a real and growing alternative for operator-investors

**Fund-of-funds**
- Check size: $5M–$200M (allocations to other funds)
- Don't invest directly in companies; invest in VC/PE funds
- Examples: Adams Street, HarbourVest, Cendana
- LPs to many of the funds above

**Secondary funds**
- Buy existing positions from LPs who want liquidity
- Examples: StepStone, Ardian, Industry Ventures
- Increasingly important as IPOs slow

---

**Stage 6: Specialty / alternative capital**

**Venture debt**
- Check size: $1M–$50M
- What it is: loans (not equity) to VC-backed companies
- Examples: Hercules Capital, Trinity Capital, Stifel, ex-SVB lenders
- Used for: extending runway without dilution

**Revenue-based financing**
- Check size: $50K–$5M
- Repaid as a percentage of monthly revenue
- Examples: Clearco, Pipe, Wayflyer (inventory/marketing focused)
- Critical for DTC, e-commerce, SaaS

**Working capital / inventory financing**
- Examples: Crayhill, Stenn, Kanmon
- Funds inventory purchases without giving up equity

**Crowdfunding (equity)**
- Examples: Republic, Wefunder, StartEngine
- Open to non-accredited investors (under SEC Reg CF)
- Mostly used by consumer brands, niche tech, and adverse-selected deals

**Hedge funds (private investments)**
- Increasingly write private checks alongside their public positions
- Examples: Tiger Global, Coatue, Whale Rock, Citadel, Point72

---

**The key distinction: VC vs. PE — what's actually different**

This is the part most people get wrong. Let me make it concrete:

| | VC | PE |
|---|---|---|
| **Target company** | Pre-revenue to growth-stage, unprofitable | Mature, profitable, often $20M+ EBITDA |
| **Stake taken** | Minority (5–25%) | Usually majority/control (51%+) |
| **Source of returns** | Power-law winners (1 deal returns the fund) | Operational improvement + financial engineering |
| **Use of leverage** | None — pure equity | Heavy debt (often 50–70% of purchase price) |
| **Hold period** | 7–12 years | 4–7 years |
| **Number of deals per fund** | 25–60 | 8–15 |
| **Risk per deal** | High (most go to zero) | Lower (most return capital, few zeros) |
| **IRR target** | 25%+ at the fund level | 15–25% at the fund level |
| **Multiple target** | 3x+ at fund level (driven by 100x outliers) | 2–3x at fund level (steady wins) |
| **Industry focus** | Tech-heavy, growth-oriented | Almost any industry with predictable cash flow |
| **Diligence depth** | Founder + market thesis | Financial models, debt schedules, operational audits |

**The key conceptual difference:** VC underwrites *uncertainty about whether a market will exist*. PE underwrites *certainty about cash flows and a clear playbook to grow them*. They're solving different problems with different tools.

---

**How a single company moves through the stack**

Real example. Take Glossier (consumer beauty brand, instructive case):

1. **2014: Founder savings + friends/family** — Emily Weiss bootstraps Into The Gloss blog → product idea
2. **2014: Seed round, $2M** — Forerunner Ventures (consumer-specialist VC) leads
3. **2016: Series B, $24M** — Thrive Capital, Forerunner, Index Ventures
4. **2019: Series D, $100M at $1.2B valuation** — Sequoia leads, becomes unicorn
5. **2021: Series E, ~$80M at $1.8B** — peak valuation
6. **2022–2023: Layoffs, struggles, no exit happens**
7. **2025: Quiet decline, valuation cut in private secondaries to ~$500M**
8. **Future likely path**: acquired by L'Oréal, Estée Lauder, or Unilever for $400–800M (consumer PE/strategic territory)

Notice: Glossier moved through three distinct types of capital (consumer-specialist seed → multi-stage growth VC → late-stage VC) and will likely exit to either a strategic or consumer PE. **Different capital at different stages, different goals at each stage.**

Now compare to Anduril (defense aerospace):

1. **2017: Founder capital + Founders Fund seed** — Palmer Luckey, Trae Stephens
2. **2018: Series A** — Founders Fund, Andreessen Horowitz
3. **2019–2024: Multiple growth rounds** — Founders Fund, a16z, Valor Equity
4. **2025: $2.5B raise at $30B valuation** — sovereign wealth + crossover funds (Founders Fund, Fidelity, BlackRock)
5. **Future likely path**: IPO at $50–100B+ valuation OR strategic acquisition by a defense prime (Lockheed, RTX)

Notice: Anduril went through fewer types of capital (almost entirely venture and growth), and its exit is more likely to be IPO than strategic. Different category, different math.

---

**Why this matters for your strategy**

Three takeaways for your trajectory:

**1. The capital landscape is a tree, not a list.** Knowing where to send a founder requires diagnosing what kind of business they're actually building. A SaaS founder pitches VCs. A beverage founder pitches consumer-specialist PE/family offices. A defense founder pitches American Dynamism funds. Sending them to the wrong vehicle wastes everyone's time.

**2. Your future fund will operate in a specific slice of the stack.** When you raise Fund I in 8–10 years, you'll be a sector-specialist VC (aerospace + AI-native physical industries). You'll compete with American Dynamism funds (a16z, 8VC, Lux, Founders Fund's defense practice). You won't compete with PE, growth equity, or consumer-specialist VCs because they're playing different games. **Knowing your position on the map is half the strategy.**

**3. OSA itself is in a specific bucket.** OSA Connect (charter brokerage) is a software-enabled services business with marketplace dynamics. As a venture investment, it would normally be backed by vertical SaaS VCs or marketplace specialists. But you're bootstrapping, which means OSA's "investors" are operating cash flow + your own equity. **You've effectively chosen the lower-middle-market PE-style path** (build a profitable cash-generating business, exit to strategics or stay independent forever). That's a deliberate choice and an unusual one for an AI-native platform — most would raise venture. The bootstrap path keeps optionality and avoids the venture treadmill but caps your scale and timeline. Worth being clear-eyed about.

---

**The thing nobody tells you**

The lines between these categories are blurring fast.

- **Tiger Global** acted like a VC for 5 years (2020–2024), then retreated to growth/public after losses
- **a16z** is increasingly hedge-fund-like in scale and structure
- **Blackstone** has a venture arm now
- **Sequoia** restructured into a permanent capital vehicle (no fund cycle)
- **Family offices** are increasingly direct-investing rather than going through funds
- **Search funds** are eating the lower-middle-market PE space
- **PE firms** are buying more software companies that traditionally would have been VC exits

The result: a founder in 2026 has more capital options than ever, but also more confusion about who to talk to. **Your future value as an investor and connector partly comes from helping founders navigate this map.** Knowing that a beverage founder belongs at L Catterton, not a16z. Knowing that a $5M-EBITDA aviation MRO business is a search fund target, not a venture deal. Knowing that an early-stage AI company should skip CVCs unless they need strategic distribution.

That ability to navigate is part of what makes "the operator with deep aviation knowledge AND capital structure literacy" valuable — to founders, to LPs, and eventually to your own portfolio. The map matters because it tells you where you fit, where you don't, and where the friction points are that an informed connector can solve.

This is the "be in the room without trying to compete" allocation. Realistic options:

- **Scout-fund LP positions** when invited.
- **Public market AI exposure**: NVDA, AMD, MSFT, GOOGL, plus AI ETFs (BOTZ, CHAT).

If you came to me asking "I have $200K and want to deploy it strategically over the next 12 months while I'm still building OSA," here's the realistic playbook:

**~$50K: 2–3 angel checks at $15–25K each into pre-seed aviation/aerospace AI companies via syndicates**

- $25K in ITA or PPA for broad sector exposure
- $25K in 1–2 conviction picks (RKLB, AVAV, or a defense AI play like Palantir on a dip)

**~$30K: Scout/rolling fund LP position**
- Subscribe to an AngelList rolling fund focused on either American Dynamism or vertical AI in physical industries
- See how pros pick, build relationships with their syndicate leads

**~$20K reserve for opportunistic follow-ons or surprise deals**

**Total: ~$200K deployed across 6–10 positions, balanced between concentrated bets in your edge zone (aerospace/aviation AI) and exposure to adjacent macro plays.**

This wouldn't make you rich. It would make you smart. And by year 3, you'd have:
- 5–10 angel positions with marked-up valuations
- Deep relationships with 2–4 sector-specialist funds
- A clear sense of which deal categories you have edge in vs. don't
- A track record that becomes the foundation for the much larger program post-OSA

**The honest punchline**

Most of the famous companies people would name as "aviation tech" — Joby, Anduril, Shield AI, Hadrian, Boom — are no longer accessible at angel scale. They're institutional plays at $1B+ valuations. The real angel game in your sector is the next wave: the seed and Series A companies that no one has heard of yet, founded by ex-Lockheed/SpaceX/JetBlue operators, building AI-native vertical software for aviation, defense, and adjacent physical industries.

You won't recognize most of their names today. That's the point. The investments that matter in 2034 are being founded in 2026 by people who currently have 3 employees and a deck. Your edge is being able to hear their pitch and immediately know if the aviation economics make sense — which 95% of generalist VCs cannot do.

The question isn't "what should I invest in today." It's **"how do I build the intake pipeline that brings the right deals to me, before they're priced into oblivion."** That's the dealflow problem, and it's exactly what OSA + Loud Hawk + the operator network give you, structurally.
